# Heroes Without History

## Greek Mythology and Arab Legends

**Fadel al-Rubaie**

# Introduction

## A Contribution to a Critique of the Sources Behind al-Tabari, Ibn Hisham, and al-Masʿudi’s Accounts of Arab Historical Figures

How can a hero exist without a history? Are there really heroes who have no history at all, even though we believe the stories told about them?

When we read the old Arabic chronicles, can we distinguish genuine historical reports from pure myth? Can we make that distinction in the accounts preserved by al-Tabari in *The History of the Kings and Prophets*, al-Masʿudi in *The Meadows of Gold*, Ibn al-Athir in *The Complete History*, al-Azraqi in *Reports of Mecca*, and al-Fakihi in *Reports of Mecca in Ancient and Recent Times*?

Traditional writing on ancient Arab history often describes history as being full of myths. I would argue instead that myths are full of history.

I have made this argument before, including in my book *Iram of the Pillars* (Riad El-Rayyes Books, Beirut, 2000). Here I want to take it further by looking for possible traces of the Greek myths of the Trojan Wars in stories transmitted by Arab and Muslim narrators.

Across different periods and generations, the circulation of these myths may have caused countless historical reports to become mixed with legendary images and events. Genuine historical material could then appear to be little more than myth or fable, while myths themselves could come to look like reliable history written down by trustworthy men.

If we can uncover a connection between the legendary wars of Troy and old Arab myths that later circulated as history, we may be able to examine the historical material preserved by al-Tabari, Ibn Hisham, al-Masʿudi, and other Arab and Muslim chroniclers in a new way. We may find that their narratives contain a characteristic blending of two elements: historical report and myth.

Claude Lévi-Strauss observed that anthropology has often concerned itself with societies that possess no written history but do possess rich and valuable bodies of myth. The disciplines concerned with myth and legend, still relatively new in the Arab world, should therefore examine just as closely everything connected with heroes who may never have been historical people at all.

This is the central idea of the book.

Ancient Arab history is crowded with figures who may not be historical. Yet modern Arab writers continue to reproduce these non-historical images and present them to the public as though they formed the heroic part of established history.

Consider some of the questions this raises.

Who was Imruʾ al-Qays? What evidence is there that he was really the son of a king who had been driven from his throne? What was his relationship to Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr, the man said to have been the “Wandering King”?

Who was al-Samawʾal, the Jewish poet said to have kept the armour of Imruʾ al-Qays in his care, then sacrificed the life of his eldest son rather than submit to the demands of the king of al-Hira, who ordered him to surrender the entrusted armour after Imruʾ al-Qays was killed?

Who was Sinnimar, the architect said to have built al-Khawarnaq, the legendary Arab palace? Why, after the Roman architect completed the building, did al-Nuʿman supposedly have him thrown from its roof? And why did Sinnimar speak of a secret known to him alone: a particular stone which, if moved even slightly from its place, would cause the whole palace to collapse?

Who was Jadhima al-Abrash, whom al-Tabari says was killed by al-Zabbaʾ, queen of Palmyra? Was al-Zabbaʾ really the same woman as Zenobia, the famous queen of Palmyra?

Who was Zarqaʾ al-Yamama, the woman of extraordinary eyesight? Muslim storytellers claimed that Hassan, the Yemeni king of Najran, killed her and then removed a black vein from her eyes which supposedly explained her remarkable sight.

And who was al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ?

The questions seem almost endless. Before we enter this tangled field and begin asking whether so many famous heroes and personalities really existed, we need to remove a conceptual obstacle that has troubled researchers even in some of the most careful studies.

The problem lies in treating the distinction between history and myth as though it were automatically the same as the distinction between truth and falsehood. Under this way of thinking, anything classified as historical can be placed in the category of truth regardless of its source or narrator, while anything classified as mythical can easily be dismissed as false.

This is a mistaken classification, yet it remains widespread.

For some modern writers, *mythical* simply means false or unreal, while *history* means something almost sacred and inherently trustworthy. It is possible, however, to demonstrate something very different. Within what has been dismissed as the supposedly false part of the past may lie genuine history written in a forgotten language.

Not so long ago, Lévi-Strauss sharply criticized European writers for the way they viewed societies without written history and for treating myth as a lower form of consciousness within those societies. He argued that such a classification destroys our ability to see the truth.

Myth contains history, but it speaks that history in a halting language, written in an alphabet we have forgotten and now struggle to learn. If so, then the search for the real history of our heroes may be possible only within the world of myth itself.

We must recognize that myth creates a personal history for the hero, a history that seems almost impossible for the wider community to share. Yet, unexpectedly, myth also connects this private history to a larger one: the history of the imagined society in which the storyteller places the heroic events.

That imagined society becomes the world in which the hero is forged, amid its flames, its suffering, and the cries of its victims.

The Muslim narrators, chroniclers, and jurists who transmitted this history did so within a continuing process of imagination and acceptance. Over time, that process led readers to treat the narratives of al-Tabari, al-Masʿudi, Ibn al-Athir, Ibn Hisham, and al-Azraqi as though they collectively represented the ancient past in its entirety.

Another possible way of understanding these texts gradually receded: one that might have drawn clearer boundaries between historical reports and religious storytelling.

We may reasonably suspect that the inherited methods of old Arabic storytelling, with their strict and deeply rooted conventions, encouraged this blending of the historical and the mythical. Even so, Arab and Muslim chroniclers often show a strong willingness to question the reports they transmit.

This helps explain al-Tabari’s cautious and conservative method, as well as the work of his follower Ibn al-Athir, a skeptical and critical writer who followed in his footsteps and did not hesitate to challenge even some of the most striking stories he recorded.

History may depend on heroes, but heroes do not always depend on history.

The most compelling images of such heroes—their tragic exploits, noble morality, and celebrated virtues—are often created within mythic narrative and therefore, in practical terms, outside history. Yet these figures still win people’s sympathy and admiration. They occupy a special place in popular memory, oral tradition, and the chronicles repeated from one generation to another.

As we have already noted, we know remarkably little, for example, about the figure called al-Samawʾal, who is said to have lived in the fortress of al-Ablaq at Tayma in the Arabian Peninsula as a Jewish poet of the pre-Islamic age.

No careful historian or serious scholar of early Arabic literary tradition possesses the evidence needed to conclude with confidence that al-Samawʾal ibn ʿAdiya was in fact a historical person.

Even Taha Hussein, in *On Pre-Islamic Poetry*, drawing on the theory of Margoliouth, offered us only one way of dealing with the question of al-Samawʾal’s existence: simply denying it, without presenting any evidence.

The same problem applies to Imruʾ al-Qays.

Our method will be different. We will take apart the old chronicles and myths in an attempt to understand the problem more deeply and, ultimately, to reach firmer conclusions.

The issue is not confined to the famous heroes of early Arabic literature. It also reaches into the political history of the Arabs.

Yemen, for example, is filled with supposed kings who were eventually assigned genealogies and periods of rule even though they were creations of later storytellers. In this book I will argue that many of them were invented by narrators working in the Umayyad and Abbasid periods.

Ifriqis, Dhu al-Qarnayn, and al-Raʾish, whom Wahb ibn Munabbih discusses in *The Crowns of the Kings of Himyar* and ʿUbayd ibn Shariyya al-Jurhumi in *Reports of Yemen*, were not kings of Yemen. Indeed, they were not historical figures at all. They were heroes of Yemeni and Arab popular tales.

Later genealogists boldly supplied these figures with sacred family trees of their own invention. As time passed, people came to repeat their stories as though they had been genuine Yemeni heroes.

If we carefully strip away the hard outer shell of myth, it can give us an extraordinary set of tools for reaching down into that unknown depth where the lost records of ancient Arab history lie buried.

Myth carries a coded message about a lost, forgotten, or vanished history. Our ancestors left such messages throughout the chronicles, from al-Tabari, al-Fakihi, al-Azraqi, ʿUbayd, and Wahb down to Ibn al-Athir, whom Nöldeke praised highly and regarded as an honest historian and a bold critic of historical reports in *The Princes of Ghassan*.

My view of Jawad Ali is very different. In *The Detailed History of the Arabs before Islam*, I see him as a writer heavily dependent on al-Tabari.

This impression arises, above all, from the haste with which Arab historians have often passed judgment on mythic narratives without developing new, revealing, and critical ways of reading them.

Myth, then, is a coded message left to us by our ancestors with remarkably little alteration. Following Erich Fromm in *The Forgotten Language*, we must learn the alphabet of myth if we hope to understand what that message contains.

Doing so may require us to remove its thick outer covering carefully and gently, open the envelope, and decipher the secret symbols inside.

Because myth carries an important message sent down to us from the past, even though we know very little about its contents and can hardly identify its true sender—whether an individual or some early human community—we have an important role to play as modern readers in passing that message on, again and again, to later generations.

Remarkably, every generation may discover something in it that the one before failed to see.

As we compete to understand the message more deeply, our greatest hope should lie less in discovering the identity of the original sender than in understanding the intended recipient. What matters is that the message should fulfil the purpose for which it was first created.

Some of these messages concern lost history, or history about which we know very little. Others belong to the collective images through which societies imagine their heroes.

An old Arab legend preserved by al-Azraqi in *Reports of Mecca*, by al-Fakihi in *Reports of Mecca in Ancient and Recent Times*, and also by al-Tabari and al-Masʿudi in *The Meadows of Gold*, tells the following story.

Many years after Abraham had sent his son Ishmael away, he decided to visit him. He set out for Mecca and, after an exhausting journey, arrived in the holy city disguised as an elderly stranger and traveller.

Ishmael was not at home.

When Abraham asked Ishmael’s wife where he was, she answered rather coldly, “He is out hunting.”

The stranger then asked whether she could offer him food and shelter.

She replied harshly that they had neither.

Her answer was cold and inhospitable. Abraham therefore decided to leave, but before doing so he entrusted her with a coded message for his son:

“Tell him to change the threshold of his house.”

When Ishmael returned and received the message, he immediately understood its true meaning. He “changed the threshold of his house”—that is, he divorced the wife who had failed to show hospitality to the guest who was, unknown to her, his own father disguised as an elderly traveller.

The legend continues.

Ishmael later married a woman from the tribe of Jurhum and continued to hope that his father would visit him. Abraham eventually returned, once again making the difficult journey to Mecca in search of his son.

Once again, Ishmael was away hunting.

This time his new wife welcomed the stranger warmly. She apologized for her husband’s absence and offered Abraham water, food, and shelter.

Delighted by her hospitality, Abraham again left a coded message for his son, but with one crucial change:

“Tell him to keep the threshold of his house firmly in place.”

The meaning was clear. Ishmael should keep his new wife, because a wife is the threshold of a man’s house.

These coded messages pass from father to son even though the two never meet face to face. In much the same way, our own ancestors have sent messages to us through myth.

We can still receive them in legends, stories, and proverbs. They often ask something of us: perhaps to change the thresholds of our own houses, perhaps to strengthen them, so that our lives and values can be put in order.

Who among us would not want to receive such a symbolic message from the distant past—a message that revealed some terrible and destructive mistake in our life and showed us what needed to be put right?

I discussed this legend in my more recent book *The Sisters of Quraysh* (Riad El-Rayyes Books, 2002).

Seen in this way, myths contain a series of enigmatic messages sent by our ancestors. We must decipher them and study their special alphabet carefully if we hope to read them properly and constructively.

Such a reading should eventually allow us to distinguish, within the historical narratives of Arab and Muslim chroniclers, invented heroes from genuinely historical ones—in other words, to separate reliable historical reports from myth.

When we search for heroes without history in order to record their stories more reliably and present them again to modern readers, this distinction is the heart of the matter.

Our purpose is not to hunt down heroes without history so that we can remove them from popular memory or destroy the fascination and greatness surrounding them. There would be no good reason to strip these figures of the symbolic values their stories have accumulated.

Quite the opposite.

The search requires us to enter deeply into the old chronicles. We do so partly out of respect for the writers who exhausted themselves collecting, investigating, and recording every detail they could find. But we also do it out of respect for the images and symbols that took shape over the centuries in Arab consciousness and came to form a symbolic history of their own.

When we turn to the Greeks, we find that the hero was imagined as a divine and exalted being, supernatural and exceptional. In the earliest poetic epics, the hero could even be imagined in the form of a serpent.

Within this primordial image, the first hero appeared as a man capable of growing from the earth as a plant grows.

The ancient Greeks seem to have inherited this image from their Phoenician predecessors—the Banū Qayn of the Arabic chronicles—who brought gods and myths to them from across the sea, from the Arabian Gulf according to the investigations attributed here to Herodotus.

Among these myths, in all likelihood, was the story of the ancient Arab deity **N**, or the fish-man of Sumerian and Babylonian belief, the first hero to bring civilization onto dry land. In Islamic tradition, the author argues, this figure later appeared under the names Noah, Enoch, Jonah, and Idris.

According to James Frazer (1854–1941), ancient mythology commonly gives a universal character to human figures “born from the earth,” such as the Babylonian Tammuz and the Egyptian Osiris, carrying them beyond the local cultures that first produced their heroic images.

At the moment when such figures emerge from the earth—the moment of their symbolic birth—they are often unable to walk properly or suffer from some form of lameness.

Achilles, the Greek hero of the Trojan War, provides the famous example. Human weakness reached him in the lower part of his leg, giving rise to the well-known image of the “Achilles heel.”

The ancient Mesopotamian *Epic of Gilgamesh* gives us a parallel image in the figure of Enkidu.

Before Enkidu is symbolically born as a hero in Uruk, where he will first challenge Gilgamesh and then become his companion and friend, he passes through a moment of physical weakness. He has only just left the wilderness, where he lived among the animals. Now he enters the city, drinks in a tavern, and sleeps with the woman there, only to find that he can no longer run after the animals as he once did.

This is the hero’s symbolic birth.

His weakness or lameness marks his passage from the primitive animal state into the human one—from nature into culture. It prepares the way for his rebirth as a human hero possessing something divine, transcendent, and exceptional.

The ancient Arabs imagined something very similar in the story of ʿAmr ibn Hind, the legendary king of al-Hira whose name, according to the author, also appears in the Talmud.

The jinn abducted him, and he lived in the wilderness among the animals. Eventually he returned to his kingdom after a chance encounter in a tavern with two comic drinking companions, or jesters, who fed him while a tavern girl gave him wine.

In my view, this legend derives from the *Epic of Gilgamesh*.

As we shall see, heroes without history repeatedly make this same journey: from nature into culture, from a pre-human stage into a human one, before finally appearing in a superhuman form.

The Arabs knew Greek myths closely during the heroic age of the Greco-Persian wars, around 300 BC. In the final chapter of this book, I will argue that the remote origins of Greek mythology were, according to Herodotus, Phoenician—which in my argument means Arab—and that they came by sea through the Arabian Gulf.

The myths therefore migrated and spent a long period away from their original home before eventually returning to their historical homeland, where they were told as strange, foreign, fascinating tales.

We do not know precisely how Greek Trojan myths entered the historical narratives of the Arabs. But through careful analysis, and by taking apart the traditional narrative structures of these chronicles, we can identify Greek mythic elements and isolate their components.

The examples that follow will allow us to examine these elements more closely and, later, to understand their symbolic messages more deeply.

Let us begin with one particular account: the building of the Quba Mosque, described here as the first mosque built in early Islam.

It is one of the best-known stories in the written history of Islam. Later narrators repeated it again and again, drawing on Ibn Hisham’s *Life of the Prophet*, al-Tabari’s *History of the Kings and Prophets*, and the other familiar Islamic sources.

According to these accounts, when the Prophet Muhammad entered Medina, he allowed his she-camel to walk freely.

The victorious Muslims desperately hoped that the camel would kneel beside one of their homes, giving them the honour of welcoming their sacred guest. To prevent one Muslim from receiving an honour denied to another, the Prophet ordered that the camel be left to choose its own way. He would stay as a guest wherever it finally stopped.

The Muslims therefore followed the camel’s footsteps, each hoping that the great honour would fall to him.

As the familiar story tells it, the camel finally knelt in a yard used for drying dates that belonged to two orphan boys of the Banū al-Najjar. The first mosque in Islam was then built on the place where the camel had knelt.

A closer examination of this account raises a number of questions about the work of the early narrators, both as historians and as literary storytellers.

They told powerful and memorable stories, some of which may never have happened in any known period of Islamic history.

There is no doubt that Ibn Hisham, Ibn Ishaq, Ibn al-Kalbi, al-Waqidi, and others were among the Muslim transmitters whose narrative methods, accuracy, and reliability were frequently questioned. The same applies to al-Masʿudi and, to some extent, al-Tabari.

In the case of the camel story, which we will examine separately later, there is no reliable historical evidence confirming that the event took place. Some of the narrators from whom the story was originally transmitted were themselves accused of weakness as sources and perhaps even of fabrication.

The deeper cultural origin of this story—a she-camel allowed to wander freely, followed by the Prophet and a crowd of Muslims, with his first mosque then built at the place where the animal stops—is, I would argue, closely connected with an ancient Greek myth from the world of the Trojan Wars.

That Greek myth, in turn, has Phoenician roots, which in my argument means that its deepest origins are ancient and Arab.

The related Phoenician legend is the story of Cadmus and Europa:

> The god Zeus abducted a mortal woman, Europa, daughter of Libya. Her brother Cadmus therefore set out to rescue her.<sup>1</sup>
>
> While Cadmus—Qudama—was searching for his sister Europa, he learned that his mother, Telephassa, had died. He temporarily abandoned his search in order to bury her.
>
> Soon afterwards, when Cadmus resumed his search for Europa, the gods instructed him to follow a particular sacred cow with special characteristics.<sup>2</sup>
>
> The gods told Cadmus to walk behind the cow and follow its path. Wherever it eventually stopped, he was to build a sacred city: Thebes. He was then to offer the cow as a sacred sacrifice to the goddess Athena.

That, in very brief form, is the original account of the ancient Greek myth whose echo, I believe, can be heard in Arabic chronicles and in the *Life of the Prophet*.

One possibility is that the myth reached the Arabian Peninsula through the trade conducted by Quraysh from the time of Qusayy, an ancestor of the Prophet. Merchants carried stories and traditions from one people to another.

The history of Islam itself lends some support to this possibility. The merchants and leading men of Quraysh opposed the Prophet’s preaching and the Qurʾanic stories he recited—stories of Moses, Jonah, Noah, and others—by claiming that they possessed stories more entertaining than those told by the Prophet.

This would mean that, through trade, the merchants and notables of Quraysh had become familiar with many myths belonging to other peoples.

There is another possibility.

Arab tribes may have known this myth, along with other Greek myths, much earlier, going back to the heroic age of the Greco-Persian wars. These wars stirred the Arab imagination deeply.

It was an age in which Greek culture spread across much of the ancient world, especially with the conquests of Alexander the Great in Iran and Asia.

So great was Alexander’s fame that some Arab chroniclers eventually claimed that Alexander—whom they identified with Dhu al-Qarnayn—had been a king of Yemen.

As Saʿid ibn Nashwan al-Andalusi put it, Alexander’s fame travelled so widely that different peoples competed to claim him as one of their own. Some Arabs therefore came to imagine that he had been an ancient king belonging to them. Al-Andalusi discusses this in *The Ecstasy of Delight in Accounts of the Arabs before Islam*.

The age of the Greco-Persian wars was genuinely heroic and continued to fire the Arab imagination for a long time.

Some Arab tribes became directly involved in those conflicts. Yemeni tribes in particular may have fought against the Greeks alongside the Persians. Persian history knows of Arab units fighting beside Persian forces.

The Greeks themselves applied their famous term “barbarians” to such peoples, a disparaging expression used by Athenian speakers for groups they regarded as lying outside their own civilized world and who came to confront them on the frontiers of Asia.

The author argues that this insulting label soon became the name of branches of Yemeni tribes which later carried it with them when they migrated to North Africa. These people are now known as the Berbers, and this, he argues, explains the linguistic similarities he sees between Amazigh and Himyaritic.

Some of the Arab tribes that took part in the wars of this heroic age later broke away and migrated towards North Africa. They appear to have retained the insulting label as a name of their own.

History offers other examples of such a process.

Early Arab authorities such as al-Kalbi and Ibn Hisham held that the name **Nizar**, applied to the northern Adnanite Arabs, had originally been an insulting label used by Persian rulers for Hijazi tribes under their authority in Bahrain. In their account, it meant “weak” or “emaciated,” deriving from the Arabic root *n-z-r*, meaning “little” or “scarce.”

It is striking that the Arab tribes who fought alongside the Persians during this heroic age seem to have developed an enduring appetite for stories of courage and chivalry.

They satisfied it through myths, heroic narratives, and legends that spread with extraordinary speed.

This process went so far that storytellers created a Yemeni Alexander identified with Dhu al-Qarnayn, as well as the figure of Ifriqis.

Wahb ibn Munabbih claims in *The Crowns of the Kings of Himyar* that Ifriqis gave Africa its name and that he had been a great king of Yemen.

Yemeni chroniclers also agreed in placing Dhu al-Qarnayn and Ifriqis in the same period. In my view, this supports the hypothesis mentioned above: that branches of Yemeni tribes known by the insulting name “barbarians” migrated to North Africa sometime after 300 BC and carried with them memories of warfare involving one of Alexander the Great’s leading commanders, Aemilianus, or Ifriqiyus.

His name and image were later transformed and Arabized in Yemeni chronicles into **Ifriqis, king of Yemen**. See Ibn al-ʿIbri, *A Short History of the Dynasties*.

Within this framework, we can begin to imagine the routes by which the myths of this Arab heroic age spread.

Their clearest expression, I would argue, is the Arab Troy: the **Basus War**.

I regard the Basus War as an Arab, Bedouin literary recreation of the Greek Trojan epic.

In other words, the Basus War became a popular desert version of the Trojan Wars.

Greek culture spread powerfully during this heroic period, and its influence travelled so widely that different peoples competed to claim its heroes.

As Ibn Nashwan al-Andalusi observed in *The Ecstasy of Delight*, every nation wanted to believe that Alexander belonged to its own lineage and had been one of its kings.

The Yemenis did exactly this. They inserted Alexander into their genealogies. Even a great scholar of Arab lineage such as al-Hamdani made what I regard as a serious mistake by assigning Dhu al-Qarnayn an ancient Himyaritic genealogy in *al-Iklil*.

With the rise of Islam, these stories and myths continued to be told at merchants’ resting places as the “tales of the ancients,” to use the Qurʾanic expression,<sup>3</sup> and in the gatherings of Quraysh and their famous political and literary council, Dar al-Nadwa.

A new generation of Arab Muslim storytellers then emerged.

Their memories and writings contained myths, legends, traditional narratives, and genuine historical reports side by side. They began to retell all this material within historical narratives describing the “history of the Arabs,” beginning with the creation of the world, passing through the age of the Greco-Persian wars, and ending with the birth of Islam.

Within this vast history, told through an endless chain of reports, stories, myths, and legends, an extraordinary and continuous blending took place. Images, ideas, words, names, countries, beliefs, and myths became intertwined.

When texts about the history of Islam and its great founder, Muhammad ibn ʿAbd Allah, were eventually written down in Mecca, older symbols and images were systematically displaced by new ones given an Islamic form.

At that point, Cadmus returned to what the author regards as his Arab roots.

He reappeared as a son of Ishmael named **Qudama**, entering the northern Adnanite genealogies as a remote ancestor of the Prophet Muhammad.

The narrator of the story of the she-camel and the building of the Medina mosque took a genuine historical report and gave it a mythical dimension. In effect, he replaced an older image with a new one. Since the Prophet was a descendant of Ishmael—an indirect son, so to speak—he could take the place of Qudama. The sacred cow was replaced by the she-camel, and the mythical event came to seem as though it had actually taken place in early Islam. When Ibn Hisham, al-Tabari, and others passed the story on, none of the Muslim narrators appears to have doubted either that the event happened or what it meant.

What makes this parallel so striking is the way cultural memories can survive over time and move from one place to another. They can become absorbed into a new setting until they seem to belong there naturally, without anyone consciously deciding to transfer or combine them. Here we find an extraordinary similarity between the name of the sacred city of Thebes—*Tayba* in Arabic—which Cadmus, or Qudama, was said to have founded, and the name associated with the city the Prophet entered in triumph and where he built the first mosque of Islam. That city was Yathrib, later known simply as Medina, but one of its old Arabic names was also *Tayba*.

Ibn Hisham even relates in the *Sira* a dream experienced by the Prophet’s grandfather, Abd al-Muttalib ibn Manaf, while he was sleeping in the courtyard of the Kaaba. In the dream, an angel of the Lord told him to dig *Tayba*. When Abd al-Muttalib asked, “What is Tayba?”, he immediately understood that he was being told to dig a well for the Arabs whose water would become among their most sacred, pleasant, and abundant: Zamzam.

According to this account in Ibn Hisham, then, an old Arab name for Zamzam was *Tayba*. And we know from ancient Egyptian history that Thebes—again, *Tayba* in Arabic—was the capital of the Hyksos, whom the author identifies as Arab Bedouin groups that entered Egypt around 1720 BCE.

This remarkable correspondence between an account from the early Islamic period and the substance of a Greek myth connected with the Trojan cycle reveals something that is central to our argument: the way elements of historical reports can pass into legends and myths, and the way the two can become entangled.

If we examine the myth of the abduction of Europa, daughter of Libya, and the founding of the sacred Greek city of Thebes, where Cadmus followed the tracks of a cow, we are led deeper into the meanings and symbols involved. The cow serves as a sacrificial link between human beings and the divine. It becomes a symbolic substitute for the dead mother and the abducted sister, carrying with it the hope of their deliverance.

This redemptive quality is what distinguishes the hero from ordinary people. It separates him from the merely human and raises him above his peers. Yet this also sharpens the contradiction at the heart of the heroic image. The hero is human by origin, like everyone else, while at the same time he rises above what is ordinary and earthly. Myth never fully resolves this contradiction. That is why heroes are so often portrayed as beings who are half human and half divine.

There can be little doubt that the founding of sacred Thebes, first among the Hyksos and later among the Greeks, corresponds within the language of religious symbolism to the establishment of the first mosque in Islam, which the author identifies here as the mosque of Medina. In one case the sacred structure takes the form of a city where people gather; in the other, it takes the form of a mosque where people assemble.

In both cases, an animal—the sacred sacrifice—plays a decisive part in identifying the holiest point in the landscape.

Human beings do not choose the sacred spot themselves and then declare it holy, mark its boundaries, and surround it with signs. The sacrificial animal does this for them. By moving and then coming to a stop, it establishes the limits of sacred space.

In both the Arab-Islamic account and the Greek myth, the animal sacrifice performs exactly the role expected of it. It walks towards a place and identifies for human beings the spot of greatest holiness and purity, so that they may build there a mosque, a temple, or a sacred city.

We should also notice what the author sees as a meaningful correspondence in the words used by the two traditions. The Arabic word *madina*, “city,” is built on the three-letter root *d-y-n*, while *masjid*, “mosque,” comes from a root associated with submission and prostration. In the narrator’s cultural memory, therefore, the building of the first mosque in Islam had to become associated with something extraordinary, miraculous, and beyond the ordinary: a sacred animal, a sacrifice, stopping at precisely the place chosen for the sanctuary.

The construction of a temple, mosque, or sacred city thus becomes connected in some way with a miracle. This visible selection of the boundaries of the sacred place suggests divine intervention through the sacrificial animal. God himself, in effect, chooses the place where the offering will be accepted.

Ancient Arabs held the she-camel in a special degree of reverence. In the author’s reading, it becomes the symbolic Arab equivalent of the Greek sacred cow in al-Tabari’s account. This reverence became so strong that the Arabs developed a special system of taboos around certain animals. Such a camel could not be slaughtered, and in some cases women were forbidden to eat its meat.

At other times, special rituals were established in which a community could gather ceremonially to slaughter a camel and eat its flesh. Early Islamic reports and legal debates preserve a whole series of terms connected with these practices, including the *saʾiba*, the *bahira*, the *hami*, and the *fasil*. Together they formed part of a firm and coherent religious and cultural system governing the Bedouin’s relationship with his animals.

The system of the *saʾiba* meant allowing a she-camel to wander freely over the land without human or physical obstruction. It had been released as an offering to the gods. It could go to water or pasture wherever it wished, and it was forbidden to slaughter it, eat its flesh, or ride it.

The she-camel of the prophet Salih, the prophet of Thamud, belonged to this same category of sacred animals.

When the narrator told the story of the Medina mosque, the author argues, he revived an ancient image deeply rooted in his culture: the sacred *saʾiba* camel dedicated by the Arabs to their gods. He then created a striking correspondence between two images—the Prophet’s camel as he entered Medina and the older sacred camel allowed to wander freely as part of an ancient religious custom.

In other words, the narrator reimagined the real image before him: the Prophet riding his camel. He then absorbed that image into the structure of a Greek myth which, the author believes, he must at some point have heard being told in the gatherings of Qurayshi merchants.

The ancient Arab story that most vividly expresses the reverence surrounding this animal is the legend of the Basus War. It does so with unusual compression and poetic force.

The author regards the Basus War as non-historical. So far, he argues, we have no firm evidence that it was a real historical event experienced by Arab tribes at any particular time. Nor, in his view, is there even weak evidence locating it in a known place or plausible period.

That differs from other historical Arab battles and wars whose memory was preserved through identifiable places, names, dates, poems, and locations. The battles traditionally known as the *Ayyam al-Arab*, the “Battle Days of the Arabs,” for example, were real conflicts remembered by tribal tradition in poetry, stories, legends, and place-names.

The “heroes” of the Basus War, by contrast, remained imagined heroes: Kulayb, Jassas, and al-Zir Salim. In an earlier study, the author argued that al-Zir Salim was a Bedouin transformation of the Egyptian myth of Osiris. See *The Devil and the Throne: The Prophet Solomon’s Journey to Yemen*, Riad El-Rayyes Books, 1996.

According to the legend, the Basus War began with the killing of a she-camel belonging to a woman called al-Basus, the maternal aunt of Jassas. The author points out that even the name *al-Basus* is strange and unusual in Arabic naming patterns.

The legend says:

> Al-Basus owned a she-camel with unusual qualities. She came to visit the encampment of her nephew Jassas, who was a rival of his cousin Kulayb, prince of Rabiʿa. Kulayb happened to see the she-camel wandering inside land that he had placed under his protection. He took an arrow and killed it. Al-Basus came weeping and demanded that her nephew avenge the animal. Jassas swore that he would take revenge for her, and this was how the terrible war began—a war said to have lasted around forty years.

The idea that an entire tribe might be destroyed because a sacred she-camel had been killed lies, the author argues, at the heart of ancient Arab belief. The obvious parallel is the tribe of Thamud, which according to tradition was utterly destroyed after killing the sacred she-camel of the prophet Salih.

This same belief lay behind the *saʾiba*: the dedication of a camel to the gods, together with a ban on slaughtering it, eating its meat, riding it, or making use of its milk. Islam later abolished this system through explicit Qur’anic legislation.

The eruption of violence, the spreading tragedy, and a war continuing for so many years amid poems, stories, and legends inevitably recalls the Trojan epic, where violence breaks out and becomes impossible to control.

This supposedly historical war, said to have begun because a sacred camel allowed to wander freely was killed, is, in the author’s view, an Arab Bedouin echo of the Trojan War. It is a re-creation of Troy shaped by a heroic age that fired the Arab imagination and encouraged endless tales of bravery and heroic deeds.

The author has no doubt that, as the Arabs told stories about other peoples—the Persians, Romans, and Indians, all peoples with whom Arab tribes came into direct contact through trade and war—they gradually came to imagine that certain stories, heroes, place-names, and battles belonged to their own ancient past.

Originally, however, some of these belonged to other peoples.

Over time they became embedded in popular collective memory until they felt intimate and unmistakably Arab. We will see the same process, the author says, in the example of Alexander the Great, or Dhu al-Qarnayn, whom Yemeni tribes came to imagine as an ancient king of Yemen.

The Basus War therefore becomes, in this reading, little more than a popular Bedouin version of the Trojan War. It tells of an extraordinary conflict among Arab tribes lasting some forty years, destroying crops, animals, and human beings alike, while its heroes rise in poetry to a remarkable height where tragedy blends with humiliation, shame, disappointment, and the thirst for revenge.

The Trojan War itself, in the author’s account, was likewise a mythical war nurtured by the heroic age of the Greek-Persian conflicts, an age that stirred the imagination of the entire ancient world.

He further argues that modern studies of ancient Greek literature have made it very plausible that Homer, the blind poet, was not a historical person at all but a legendary figure, and that the *Iliad* was assembled from older poems whose original forms were lost, together with traditions and stories that may even have preceded the emergence of the Greek people themselves.

To pursue the questions at the heart of this book, the author therefore believes that he must put forward several bold conclusions about the Arab retelling of the mythical Trojan War through its popular Bedouin version, the Basus War.

He also hopes to encourage Arab critics, literary scholars, writers, researchers, and historians to engage in a calm and serious discussion of the figure and poetry of al-Aʿsha. In the historical reports, al-Aʿsha became associated with the telling of heroic and epic Arab poetry in a way comparable to Homer.

The author of *al-Muʾtalif wa-l-Mukhtalif* mentions two poets called al-Aʿsha. One certainly belonged to the pre-Islamic period, while the other lived into the Islamic age. But ancient Arab writers, the author argues, failed to notice a third al-Aʿsha—an invented and imaginary figure to whom stories and poems were attributed through deliberate fabrication.

What Arab literary history knows as al-Aʿsha, or *Sannajat al-Arab*, is therefore, in the author’s view, essentially an Arab imitation of the Homeric figure associated with the Greek epics.

There was no single poet or historical person of exactly the kind later sources presented under the name al-Aʿsha. The poetry attributed to him—poetry telling the epics, stories, and myths of the Arabs, including the tales of Jadhima, al-Zabbaʾ, Sinimmar, and the marriage of al-Nadira daughter of al-Dayzan—was, the author argues, composed by later Muslim narrators and falsely attributed to a much earlier figure on the basis of older sources that have since disappeared.

To avoid either deliberate or innocent misunderstanding, he stresses one further point. The uncertain genealogy of the pre-Islamic poet known as al-Aʿsha, as distinct from another poet of that name who lived into Islam, together with the confusion among Arab and Muslim narrators over the period in which he actually lived, brings the whole matter back under examination: the title, the name, and the historical period.

The word *sannaja*, the author argues, does not mean a musical instrument. It describes a kind of singing. This fits precisely the type of poetry attributed to al-Aʿsha: lyrical verse that tells the heroic stories, legends, and epics of the Arabs.

This sung poetry associated with al-Aʿsha parallels the kind of performance traditionally connected with Homer, the legendary Greek poet who recited myths and heroic epics in long and compelling poems.

Homer was also said to have been blind. He sang of heroic deeds in the midst of wars he had never seen, though they moved him deeply. The Arabs likewise made their poet al-Aʿsha a man afflicted by defective sight, since the Arabic word *aʿsha* refers to a person suffering from a form of night blindness.<sup>4</sup>

These similarities, the author argues, cannot simply be dismissed as coincidence. They deserve careful thought for both their literary and historical significance.

When peoples preserve heroic traditions through poetry, they tend to attribute those poems to a particular poet. Just as the Greeks attributed their epic poetry to the blind Homer, the Arabs attributed their own ancient heroic verse to a blind poet, al-Aʿsha.

The author suggests that this may also connect with old religious beliefs that gave a sacred quality to unusual or miraculous forms of sight and insight.

Ancient communities—Persians, Arabs, Greeks, Indians, and others—did not merely create non-historical heroes. They also created the poets who sang of those heroes.

Just as the Greek people created Heracles, the legendary hero, together with the blind Homer as the poet of heroic exploits and epics, so the Arabs created Jadhima, Sinimmar, Zarqaʾ al-Yamama, and al-Nadira, and beside them their poet al-Aʿsha.

The author promises to return to these hypotheses later.

We can now return to the she-camel.

If the image imagined by Muslim storytellers—a camel wandering freely through a newly opened city while the Prophet and a group of men, joyful in victory, followed behind it—was one of several historical echoes of myths belonging originally to other peoples, then its insertion into a genuine historical account of the building of the Quba Mosque carries a particular meaning.

Through this image, the Arabs recovered the remains of an old religious belief in which an animal sacrifice performed a miraculous role as an expression of divine blessing.

Even today, the author notes, Muslims in the Levant honour a particular site in southern Syria, at Bosra, known as the “Kneeling Place of the She-Camel” (*Mabrak al-Naqa*).

He says that he once visited the site himself. It consists of a square stone with hollows in it inside the citadel of Bosra.

In reality, he says, the site is part of the remains of a Roman temple. Popular tradition, however, calls it the Kneeling Place of the She-Camel because people believe that the Prophet’s camel knelt there.

The commonly accepted Islamic story places the kneeling of the camel in ancient Yathrib, or Medina, not in Bosra. Yet some Muslims in Syria claim that this was the place where the Prophet’s camel knelt and where he dismounted and prayed.

At the same time, the Arab-Islamic sources tell us that the Prophet came to Bosra as a boy with his uncle Abu Talib as part of a trading caravan.

The fact that Arabs preserved this and similar legends, and that major Muslim historians such as al-Tabari carefully included them in accounts of the Prophet’s life without devoting serious attention to criticism of their sources or to testing their historical reliability, points, the author believes, to something deeper than a simple wish to fill the Prophet’s biography with miracles, divine signs, and other striking or magical features.

For the Arabs, stories of this kind expressed something intimate and deeply rooted in their collective emotional world as shaped by Islam.

They represented a recovery of the earliest myths—the “tales of the ancients” mentioned in Qur’an 16:24—which had wandered away, migrated, or been lost since the childhood of Arab humanity.

The Arabs then produced new popular Islamic versions of them, filled with powerful feelings about the greatness and glory of Islam.

They did so, the author argues, without any sense that they were borrowing something belonging to their Greek or Persian neighbours. On the contrary, they felt that they were recovering something that was already theirs, or making use of something shared with other peoples.

In this sense, the story of Cadmus and the cow returns, through its Arab version—the Prophet and the kneeling place of the she-camel—to what the author regards as its original historical homeland: Arabia.

These, he argues, are precisely the “tales of the ancients” referred to in the Qur’an when speaking of Quraysh’s attempt to compete with the Prophet over the new religion. Quraysh claimed that the new faith consisted merely of stories being told and ancient tales being recited in the resting places of merchants. Because they controlled the great trade routes, they believed that they too possessed a storehouse of stories capable of rivaling the Prophet’s teachings.

The depth of the Arab sense of cultural kinship with these myths, as though they formed an organic part of ancient Arab culture, together with the technical ease with which Arab narrators could incorporate them into historical storytelling, reveals something more than simple familiarity.

It reveals, the author argues, a feeling of cultural kinship with the heroes themselves.

Cadmus, in this interpretation, is Qudama, son of Ishmael, son of Abraham, prophet of the Arabs.

Europa, the beautiful abducted girl, is likewise not, in the author’s argument, a completely foreign or opaque name to an Arab ear.

Before she became “Europa, daughter of Libya,” he identifies her with *ʿAraba*, an ancient name for Arabia. In Arabic lexicography, he notes, a beautiful young woman can be described as *ʿarub* or *ʿaruba*, a word that also appears in the Qur’an.

The name *ʿAraba*, he suggests, may itself be extremely ancient. The Bible uses a related name in connection with the homeland of the Bedouin and the great desert zone extending between the southwest of the Arabian Peninsula and its northern region, the Hijaz.

The myths of the ancient world, he argues, often operated through pure symbols. Countries and people could become symbolic elements within an entire system of signs: a city could become a girl, a god could become a bull.

What is striking, in his view, is that Arab writers were aware of this and recorded it. Quoting al-Hamdani’s *Description of the Arabian Peninsula*, he notes that *ʿAraba* was said to be the old name of the Arabs’ historic homeland. Poetry attributed to Abu Talib, as well as a report from Ibn Abbas, likewise refers to the Arabs’ homeland as *ʿAraba*.

Even today, Jordan’s Wadi Araba preserves this ancient form of the name, just as the al-Qadam district in the heart of Damascus preserves, in the author’s interpretation, the name Qudama. Popular Syrian tradition says that the area was called *al-Qadam*, “the Foot,” because the Prophet’s foot once touched the place.

The Phoenicians—identified in Arab-Islamic sources by the author with the Banu Qayn, and in North Africa with a form he regards as the more correct rendering, *Biniqiyyin*—carried their earliest myths with them during their great maritime migrations towards Crete and the Mediterranean coasts.

The Greeks then retold those myths within a new Hellenic cultural setting, stripping away their earlier pastoral character.

It was the Greeks who later gave Europeans the name of their continent, the author says, just as they gave them the names of some of their capitals—Paris, for example, which was also the name of one of the heroes of Troy before becoming the name of the French capital.

When the Greek people appeared on the shores of Crete, they learned the alphabet, according to Herodotus, from the Phoenicians, the people of the sea whom the author connects with the Arabian Gulf.

Canaanite myths were then “Greekified” and absorbed into a new culture.

With the dramatic conquests of Alexander the Great, the rise of Athens in the ancient world, the spread of Hellenistic culture, and later the Greek-Persian wars in the third century BCE, these myths began to be told once again, in another language, in what the author regards as their original homelands.

Yet, remarkably, he argues, they preserved traces of their earliest material and sound patterns: Cadmus remained Qudama, and Europa remained *ʿAruba* or *ʿAraba*.

Throughout their long history, he believes, the Arabs repeatedly returned to these Trojan myths. They consciously borrowed and recovered elements from them, repeatedly blending images produced by these wars in the Bedouin imagination until those images entered the very substance of historical reports.

Among these were the many legends of the “poisoned necklace” and the “poisoned gift,” some associated with Cadmus and others with Heracles.

One of these myths says:

> After Cadmus completed the building of Thebes, he married Harmonia, daughter of Ares. The gods were not entirely pleased with the marriage, so they gave the bride an enchanted necklace as a wedding gift. The necklace soon became a curse, however, bringing disaster upon everyone who wore it.

This poisoned gift belongs to a wider series of gifts in Greek mythology in which the gods present objects to human beings, kings, or heroes.

The author believes that the distant cultural origin of these stories lies in the great Phoenician migrations from the Arabian Peninsula to Crete, which he regards as historically established. Their roots were therefore Canaanite—or, in his terminology, ancient proto-Arab—before they eventually acquired their Hellenic form and distinctively Greek character.

They later returned, he argues, in popular Bedouin versions told in Arabia.

The immediate meaning of a poisoned gift is that the alliance between gods and human beings is uncertain, dangerous, and potentially poisonous. It may contain risks that neither side expects, particularly when human actions appear to disregard divine wishes, laws, or commands.

Such alliances are repeatedly accompanied by poisonous gifts.

The author gives Heracles as another example. The gods gave the great heroic figure a garment—a golden robe—as a reward for victory and as part of the fulfilment of a difficult condition attached to his marriage to a king’s daughter. Heracles soon fell victim to the deception. The garment was poisoned, and his body was torn apart and consumed by its effects.

The author argues that Arab storytellers transferred this Trojan image of the death of Heracles almost word for word to the stories surrounding the pre-Islamic poet Imruʾ al-Qays.

They made him travel to the lands of the Romans and fall in love with the emperor’s daughter. When the affair was discovered, the emperor presented him with a poisoned robe. As soon as Imruʾ al-Qays put it on, the poison caused his flesh to rot and his body to break apart.

As the author says he has argued in his newer work, *The Invention of Imruʾ al-Qays*, the later image and poetry of Imruʾ al-Qays were assembled from the images and poems of other poets and kings.

His study of the Jewish poet al-Samawʾal, he says, points in the same direction, because Imruʾ al-Qays was never a historical person of the kind later tradition imagined. The author of *al-Muʾtalif wa-l-Mukhtalif*, he notes, lists around sixteen pre-Islamic Arab poets who bore the same name.

The stories of Imruʾ al-Qays reveal, in his view, a striking saturation with Greek and Persian stories, traditions, and myths that were circulating at the time.

Here the author draws attention to al-Tabari, whom he regards as a careful and exact historian. Through intelligence and wide learning, al-Tabari succeeded in preserving almost everything that came into his hands, whether in written form or through oral transmission.

Unfortunately, the author says, his sources in this area were largely Persian. Jawad Ali, following Nöldeke’s analysis, had already made the same point.

Al-Tabari did not seriously test these traditions against Greek sources. Some aspects of his picture of Arab history therefore deserve fresh critical examination.

Such examination would reveal not only the sources behind the blending of genuine reports with mythical images and ideas, but something more important: the way the Arab image of its own history was gradually formed during the harsh and destructive centuries of Sasanian rule.

From this perspective, al-Tabari’s enormous *History of the Prophets and Kings* can be regarded both as the most important surviving work on ancient Arab history and, at the same time, as an outstanding example of the repeated blending of historical reports with images, interpretations, inventions, and myths.

It is therefore a particularly valuable case study in the entanglement of history and mythology.

Alongside the legends of poisoned garments and the poisoned necklace, there are other myths built around the same central idea: the alliance between the divine and the human breaks down and slides towards tragedy, often ending with the human body being torn apart by poison.

Ibn Sallam, author of *al-Tabaqat*, transmitted some of these stories, as did al-Isfahani in *al-Aghani*. Like al-Tabari, the author argues, they drew on Persian material and then adapted it so that it could absorb widely circulating tribal traditions.

Al-Tabari and other Muslim chroniclers therefore surrendered, in the author’s view, more readily to the attraction and dramatic power of mythical storytelling than to the demands of history. This becomes visible when they record events from the distant past and present them as though they were sound historical reports.

From around 250 to 300 AH, an entire generation of Muslim chroniclers, jurists, linguists, and Qur’anic commentators devoted itself, following narrative models such as al-Tabari’s, to writing ancient Arab history by blending myth with historical material.

The task today, the author argues, is one of genuine historical preservation: to divide this recorded past back into its two parts and boldly re-examine it, separating myth from history.

Al-Tabari’s account of the kingdom of Hatra offers one of the clearest examples of this repeated fusion of historical and mythical material.

Hatra was a small Arab desert kingdom around 320 kilometres north of Baghdad. The author associates its founding with Azd tribes of Yemeni Qahtani stock connected with Qudaʿa. It flourished during the period of Persian dominance over Iraq, roughly between 140 and 226 CE.

Its best-known king, according to the author, was Sanatruq. Hatra courageously resisted both the Persian and Roman empires before the Sasanians finally defeated and destroyed it.

Al-Tabari tells the story of the marriage of al-Nadira, daughter of al-Dayzan, to the Persian king Shapur ibn Ardashir.

Al-Dayzan appears frequently in Arab and early Islamic traditions as one of the kings of Hatra, although, the author points out, his name does not appear in the lists of Hatra’s rulers known from archaeology.

We likewise possess no reliable historical information confirming the marriage of his daughter to Shapur. No sound Arab or Islamic historical source independently records it, nor has any mention of it been found in the inscriptions, objects, or texts uncovered by archaeologists among the ruins of Hatra.

What makes al-Tabari’s version especially interesting is that the marriage takes place during a bloody and heroic siege with a strongly tragic character.

According to the account, Shapur ibn Ardashir imposed a merciless and devastating blockade on the small desert kingdom for four full years.

Al-Tabari’s story continues:

> After four years of siege, Shapur ibn Ardashir was surprised one day to see the daughter of the besieged Arab king looking out from the walls at the Persian army. Something completely unexpected then happened. The young and handsome Persian king fell in love with the daughter of his enemy, and she in turn fell in love with the enemy of both herself and her father.
>
> Al-Nadira therefore gave the Persian king the secrets of the fortified and besieged kingdom: the magical method by which he could break its defences and enter the city in triumph. She told him that she possessed the secret capable of undoing the spells protecting the walls, but that she wanted something equally precious in return.
>
> The Persian king promised to marry her and to place her “above his other wives.”
>
> And so it happened. The Persian king returned victorious, killed the Arab king, and announced his marriage to al-Nadira. Yet the violent love affair and marriage soon collapsed. The Persian king abandoned his beloved and then killed her.

The author’s later analysis of this story, together with his examination of al-Tabari’s account of Shapur’s campaign against Hatra, will divide the material into two separate parts: the historical report and the myth.

In his view, this analysis demonstrates that the legends and stories of the Trojan War were powerfully present inside al-Tabari’s text.

The historical account of the siege of Hatra had been blended with, and effectively dissolved into, a Greek myth that was probably familiar to the people of Hatra themselves, who were known to have been influenced by Persian and Greco-Roman culture.

In other words, historical material concerning Shapur ibn Ardashir’s campaign against Hatra became fused with a Trojan-type myth in which one legendary king besieges another, the siege leads to an overwhelming love affair and marriage, and the story ends with the tragic death of the woman who betrayed her people.

As we will see in greater detail, a whole series of Greek stories and myths develops similar variations around the siege of the kingdom of Megara.

The author also draws attention to what he regards as an intriguing linguistic similarity between the Arabic word *maghara*, meaning a cave or rocky hollow, and the Greek name Megara, which he also understands in the sense of cave, cavity, or grotto.

For him, this supports the broader hypothesis that Greek myths have deep proto-Arab roots, ultimately deriving from Phoenician migrations from the shores of the Arabian Gulf to Crete, in accordance with what he describes as Herodotus’s historical theory.

Alongside the theme of one king besieging another, ancient Greek literature repeatedly explores another idea: falling in love with the enemy.

This theme was not confined to Greek culture. It appeared throughout the cultures of the region and carried a rich range of symbolic meanings.

One Trojan-related myth built around this theme is the story of Theseus and Ariadne.

Theseus was said to be the son of the dangerous sea-god Poseidon. The author pauses over the structure of the name *Poseidon*, which he regards as unusual in Greek and connects, speculatively, with the Arabic words *ab* and *abu*, “father.” Theseus was also born of a human mother.

Ariadne, meanwhile, was the daughter of Minos, son of Zeus. The author notes that he will later discuss what he sees as a connection between the Greek Minos and the Arabic Mina. Minos too was born of a human mother.

The myth says:

> While Theseus, son of Poseidon, was trapped inside a labyrinth and did not know how to escape, he saw Ariadne, daughter of King Minos, son of Zeus. They fell in love. King Minos had no idea that his daughter Ariadne had fallen in love with his enemy Theseus, who was wandering helplessly through the complicated maze with no idea how to find a way out.

The story suggests that Minos, the girl’s father, was responsible for placing Theseus inside the labyrinth.

But blind and reckless love drove Ariadne to give her lover the “end of the thread” that would guide him out—a phrase the author identifies as the source of a familiar expression in popular Arabic.

Yet this passionate love also ended unexpectedly in disaster. Theseus eventually killed his beloved.

In this version of the myth, Theseus does not kill the girl’s father, King Minos. Instead he kills the Minotaur, the mythical monster. The author treats the monster as the symbolic substitute for the father or king: the double is killed in his place.

Instead of a thread offered by Ariadne to her lover and her father’s enemy, al-Nadira, in al-Tabari’s Arab version, gives her Persian lover the secret spells that will allow him to destroy the walls of the besieged kingdom.

The author asks us to notice how the Arab narrator seems to have drawn upon linguistic associations when reshaping the story.

The Arabic word *khayt*, “thread,” leads him to a similar-sounding word, *hayt*, “wall.” The popular Arabic fascination with such sound relationships and riddles is well known.

The narrator of the Arabic mythical text, whose name al-Tabari does not give, thus constructed a parallel Arab version of the Greek story by preserving its central idea: a daughter betrays her father, then dies at the hands of her lover during a war and siege.

There is also a clear structural similarity between the labyrinth and the long, exhausting siege of the desert kingdom. Both represent a trap from which someone must escape.

In one case, a thread offers the means of escape from the labyrinth. In the other, the lover gives away the secret that allows the walls of the city to be broken.

At another symbolic level, the Arab myth, like the Greek one, tells the story of killing the father—or of killing his symbolic double. This represents what the author regards as the most radical form of breaking family bonds, what psychology might describe as a violation of blood kinship.

In the Greek story of Minos and his daughter Ariadne, the father’s death occurs only symbolically. The Minotaur, the king’s double, is killed instead of the father, who here appears in the form of a king.

This element of the myth is, in the author’s view, closely related to the idea of killing the father expressed in the story of Oedipus the King.

Al-Tabari’s story of the marriage of al-Nadira therefore becomes, in this interpretation, a systematic Arab development of the same underlying theme.

The Arabs, like the Greeks, had myths of killing the father—or parallel traditions built around the breaking of blood ties.

If we look further for these similarities among stories of heroes who possess no genuine history but nevertheless function as heroes, we find a rich body of material revealing different forms of imitation and variation upon an older model.

Another myth from the same narrative tradition says:

> King Minos was at war with King Nisus of Megara. During the bloody conflict between the two kings, Scylla, daughter of Nisus, fell in love with her father’s enemy.
>
> Minos wanted to obtain a magical golden lock of hair growing on the head of King Nisus, which protected the king from death.
>
> Scylla became the victim of her own blind and reckless love. During Minos’s siege of Nisus, she cut off the magical lock and gave it to her lover. In doing so she stripped her father of his protection. His strength left him and he became easy to kill.
>
> Minos, now victorious, killed Nisus and announced that he would marry Scylla. But events quickly moved towards violent tragedy. Minos abandoned her and declared his contempt for her because she had betrayed her father. Finally, the victorious king killed the woman who loved him.

Greek culture preserved a parallel variation in the story of Comaetho, daughter of Pterelaus, who fell in love with Amphitryon. The same basic pattern appears again: the beloved betrays her father, and the lover later kills her.

Across these stories, a young woman’s love and attachment to a man become so powerful that she betrays her own father. These literary variations on the same central idea were designed, the author suggests, to perform different functions and deliver different messages through poetic narratives.

The betrayal of a father by his daughter in pursuit of a husband, followed by the husband’s contempt, abandonment, and eventual killing of her, was widespread in the cultures of the ancient world.

So too was the theme of killing the father, which the author regards as common to all cultures.

Al-Tabari’s account, in his view, merged two separate things: the genuine historical fact of a Persian military campaign against the Arab kingdom of Hatra, ending in the destruction of the kingdom and the death of its ruler; and a mythical marriage said to have taken place during the war.

As we have seen, he considers the latter to be Greek in origin.

The narrator of the report—al-Tabari—was therefore unable to separate the historical campaign against Hatra from the old myth that had become attached to it, and came to treat the two as one story.

In fairness, however, the author stresses that al-Tabari himself did not create this fusion.

His cautious and conservative method of narration merely strengthened it by placing it into written historical form. He probably drew on Persian historical records going back to the Sasanian period.

It is also possible that al-Tabari originally heard the tale from tribal storytellers accustomed to telling this kind of ancient story, or that he encountered a much older written tribal source.

The latter is quite possible, since Arab tribes did in fact preserve records containing poetry, genealogies, and their own traditions. Qudaʿa may have been among them.

Al-Hamdani supports this possibility in *al-Iklil*, where he claims to have consulted written genealogical records. Al-Amidi likewise says in *al-Muʾtalif wa-l-Mukhtalif* that he had seen old collections of tribal poetry.

The poetic version of the story attributed to al-Aʿsha might therefore fit the author’s hypothesis that there was once an old popular tradition of telling such myths in verse, much like the Greek poetic epics.

There can also be little doubt, he argues, that the Arab version of the legend of al-Nadira reflects the longstanding tension between Arabs and Persians, with all the uneasy alliances and contradictions that accompanied their relations through many centuries.

One of the clearest expressions of this tension was the strong resistance of Arab tribes to Persian demands for marriage with Arab women.

The tribes firmly rejected repeated attempts by Persian kings to arrange such marriages.

This resistance appears clearly, the author says, in accounts of the conflict between the rulers of Persia and al-Hira surrounding what specialists in this history know as the *Baqar al-Sawad* affair.

Al-Nuʿman is said to have become furious at Khosrow’s insistence on marrying Arab women and eventually paid for his refusal with his life.

Behind this resistance lay an instinctive tribal belief that Persia sought such marriages as a way of securing permanent tribal loyalty—binding Arab ambitions and restricting their desire to establish independent kingdoms and principalities.

To prevent the Sasanians from strengthening Persian influence in the Arabian desert through marriages with Arab women, the tribes stigmatized any such approach.

Arab chroniclers even preserved a controversial story claiming that Zurara ibn ʿUdas al-Tamimi, chief of Tamim—or perhaps one of his sons, possibly Laqit—married his own daughter after adopting the Persian Zoroastrian religion.

The author notes that many sources indicate that this accusation was untrue.

The point of the alleged incestuous marriage, however, was to express tribal disgust at anyone who submitted either to Persian religion or to Persian policy.

Old accounts even preserved poems mocking Dakhantus, daughter of Laqit—or Habis—sons of Zurara, in which the supposed marriage was explicitly linked with Zoroastrianism, a religion regarded with hostility among many Arabs.

It was against this larger background that the Persian military campaign against Hatra took place.

Hatra had generally enjoyed a degree of stability because of its fairly balanced policy towards its neighbours, including the client rulers of Rome in nearby principalities.

If we examine the historical evidence for Shapur ibn Ardashir’s campaign, which Nöldeke studied in detail, the claim of a four-year siege begins to look like a later insertion.

Historically, the author argues, there was no prolonged four-year siege.

The long siege is instead a literary device designed to deepen the audience’s sense of the cruelty of the war and to place the mythical marriage at the centre of a tragedy.

Within the Arab tribal response to Persian marriage demands, the story carries a powerful moral lesson: any Arab woman who violates this prohibition and marries a Persian becomes a traitor to her father, and her marriage will end in her death.

Al-Tabari nevertheless claims that the siege of Hatra lasted four years—or, in another version, three—while the poetic account attributed to al-Aʿsha says that it lasted two.

By telling the legend of al-Nadira throughout long periods of tension with Persia, the tribes gave the Greek myth—now thoroughly Arabized—a direct moral purpose and made it appear to be a historical event.

The story warned Arabs of the importance of maintaining what the author calls a sexual prohibition against Persia.

Any Arab woman dreaming of marrying a handsome Persian prince could now be represented as a traitor like al-Nadira, deserving certain death at the hands of the man she loved.

Through literature, al-Nadira’s image was thus fixed in collective memory as though she had been a genuine historical person.

There was another, less visible dimension to this hostility: religion.

Accepting such a marriage could also mean submitting to the official religion of Persia. In the Sasanian period, the religious world of Persia included Mazdakism, while Arab tribes in al-Hira were increasingly moving towards Christianity in its Nestorian form.

Beyond the story of al-Nadira’s marriage, there is a whole series of ancient Arab myths that have repeatedly been presented to readers as historical accounts, with their heroes treated as people who genuinely lived through the events attributed to them.

One example is the figure known in Arab history books as **Abu Righal**.<sup>6</sup> He is usually presented as a traitor, although, in my view, no such person ever existed. Ibn Hisham does mention him as a traitor in his *Sirah*, but his is an isolated account, and one for which there is no sound basis.

In his brief and rather clumsy version of the Abu Righal story, Ibn Hisham claims that after the Abyssinian ruler Abraha took control of Yemen, he sent an army to destroy the Kaaba. He asked the tribe of Thaqif in Taif to provide a man who could guide him to Mecca. According to the story, this guide was called Abu Righal, but he died along the way.

Elsewhere, however, we have presented what we consider decisive evidence that ancient Arab history knew no man by this name, much less a man who served as guide to the invading army in the Year of the Elephant. Abu Righal, as a person, was an invention introduced into the Prophet’s biography by Ibn Ishaq and Ibn Hisham.

The place where Abraha’s army halted during its advance on Mecca lay in the Taif region and was known as **al-Mughammis**. In ancient times, Arabs would stone a small spot there called **Abu Righal**. The word *raghl* referred to a desert plant that people and animals ate during periods of severe famine.

The practice of throwing stones at this place existed before Abraha’s campaign. It was a ritual connected with famine and its memory. When later storytellers heard the name Abu Righal repeated in gatherings where the campaign was discussed, they imagined that Abraha must have employed a man of that name as his guide, and they wrote him into the story.

Ibn Ishaq, followed by Ibn Hisham, then deepened the confusion by merging the place and the supposed person into a single figure. From then on, readers of Arab history encountered the story of Abraha’s invasion together with the tale of an Arab traitor called Abu Righal.

Yet it makes little sense that an enormous military expedition would depend on a single guide, who then happened to die on the road. Ibn Hisham himself tells us in the *Sirah* that the Arab tribes around Taif were divided in their response to the invading force. Some members of Thaqif cooperated with it, while other tribes resisted it. In a campaign on this scale—an attempt to conquer Arabia, something Athens, Rome, and Persia had all previously failed to achieve—why would the army need the services of one solitary man when entire tribes were offering assistance?

The most likely explanation is that, after the triumph of Islam, the compilers of the old accounts deliberately concealed the real names of the tribes and leaders who had helped the Abyssinian occupiers. Some of their descendants later acquired standing within the new religion, which discouraged public disgrace and denunciation.

There was also a deeper narrative reason. Myth has a tendency to place an entire community’s guilt onto one central character. In this case, the collective guilt of the “treacherous tribes” was cast into a single figure: Abu Righal.

Another widely repeated invention concerns **Hujr**, the father of Imruʾ al-Qays. According to the story, Hujr decided to have his son killed and ordered one of his followers to carry out the execution after hearing that the poet had composed erotic verses about his father’s wives—or, according to one version, about a wife named Fatima (*al-Aghani*, al-Isfahani).

Instead of killing Imruʾ al-Qays, however, the man slaughtered a wild cow, tore out its eyes, and presented them to the king as proof that his son was dead.

The supposed reason for the murder was the poet’s sexual pursuit of his father’s wife.

This closely reproduces the Greek story associated with **Theseus** and his son **Hippolytus**. Here another pattern enters the collection of non-historical images. In the story of Hippolytus, the king openly plots to have his own son killed. The same basic narrative can also be seen in the story of Joseph son of Jacob, known in Islamic tradition and in the Qur’an as the Prophet Joseph, and told in the Torah as a historical story set in Egypt and connected with the Israelites. The Qur’an also recounts it in the chapter named after Joseph.<sup>7</sup>

The Greek myth tells the story as follows:

> Hippolytus became the target of his stepmother Phaedra, who fell in love with him and confessed her desire. Hippolytus rejected this forbidden love. Phaedra then accused the young man of attempting to assault her sexually. When King Theseus heard the accusation, he resolved to have his son Hippolytus killed and called upon Poseidon to carry out the punishment.

This myth provides an ancient pattern for two Arab stories that strongly captured the imagination of the tribes.

The first is the story of the legendary poet Imruʾ al-Qays, whose royal father became angry with him and ordered his guards to kill him. The second is the story of Joseph son of Jacob, whom Zulaykha, the wife of Potiphar, the Egyptian official, accused of sexual assault.

This pattern moves in the opposite direction from the famous Greek myth of Oedipus. It reverses the central theme. Instead of the son killing his father, the father seeks the death of the son.

This is also one of the direct themes of the Joseph story as told in the Torah, in Genesis, where Potiphar punishes Joseph, whom the narrative effectively places in the position of an adopted son, by throwing him into prison after he is accused of planning a sexual assault on the father’s wife.

The ancient Arab background to the idea of a son threatening his father’s wives can also be seen in a form of marriage that remained common in Arab society for a long time: a son marrying a woman who had been married to his father.

This practice continued until very near the rise of Islam, and Arabs did not necessarily regard it as shameful. Islam eventually prohibited it completely and unequivocally.

The practice was known as **dayzan marriage**, although the Qur’anic text refers to it as a marriage of *maqt*—a hateful or detested union. The term reflected the new Islamic rejection of customs from the pre-Islamic age under which a son could inherit not only his dead father’s property, but also his wife. The sons might compete for the father’s possessions after his death, and the eldest could throw his cloak over his father’s widow as a sign that he intended to marry her.

The pre-Islamic poet **Amr ibn Maʿdi Karib** was said to have married his father’s wife. In a famous verse he said:

> Were it not for my brothers, and my sons by her,
> I would have filled my right hand against her with a blade.

The central idea behind these stories of threatened sexual access to the father’s wives helps explain the desire to kill the son. The son is imagined as a possible rival who might threaten the father’s possession of his women while the father is still alive.

Something similar appears in the biblical story of David, whose son rebelled against him, drove him from the throne, took possession of his women, and openly slept with them before the eyes of the people.
