If we concentrate on the relationship between the Sumerian root **En** and **Nun**, both of which in our view carry the meaning of fish or whale, we remain very close to the earliest ideas surrounding the ancient god of water as they developed in Sumerian and Egyptian consciousness.

The first part of the Sumerian-Babylonian name **En-ki**, *En*, is associated with water. It points toward the development of the root *En* into the Arabic root **N**, which the Qur’anic text endowed with awe and sacred significance by linking it directly with learning, writing, and wisdom in the verse:

**“Nun, by the pen.”**

The original name, Enki, referred to a god regarded by the successive ancient cultures of Mesopotamia as a being of enormous power.

He was the first human being imagined in the shape of a fish—the first fish—who emerged from the water after overcoming its terrors and dangers, carrying with him onto dry land his great gift:

**writing and knowledge.**

A clay tablet from the dawn of Sumerian history describes a creature that is half fish and half man. He is called **O-Annes**. He emerges onto dry land after surviving the terrors of the sea, bringing civilization with him.

What is striking about this Sumerian name—which the Greeks also rendered as O-Annes—is the resemblance between a being who is human in the form of a fish and the structure of the Arabic word **anas**, the root from which **insan**, “human being,” is formed.

In our view, the name of the prophet **Yunus** in Arabic religious and historical tradition ultimately derives from this same linguistic material.

From it also comes the biblical name **Yunan**, or in Hebrew **Yonah**.

Both emerge miraculously from the water. Both survive danger and cross the sea before finally reaching dry land with a gift: the religious message.

Within this wider framework, it is important to notice that the **Thamudites** knew and worshipped a god called **Nun**. They even wrote his name in the same form in which it appears in the Qur’an: **Nun**.

F. Brandi, in *History of Thamud* (p. 57), observed that the food of this tribe was connected with the worship of the god Nun.

This, in his view, reveals something about their earlier homeland near the Red Sea coast of Arabia. Rock carvings have been found showing Thamudite boats that tribesmen used to cross the sea for trade. This supports the archaeological conclusion that at one stage the Thamudites lived on resources drawn from the Red Sea and obtained their principal food from it.

Further evidence comes from the discovery of fish bones at Thamudite sites and routes, suggesting that fish formed part of a ritual diet connected with the worship of **Nun**, who, as we have said, was one of the deities of Thamud.

The tribe therefore combined two traditional ways of life.

One was Bedouin nomadism, with all its hardship, migration, and constant movement in search of food.

The other was settlement along the coast.

This alone helps explain the strange old Arab proverb:

**“Like one who combines the desert lizard with the nun.”**

The proverb had already become difficult to understand in the pre-Islamic age.

The **dabb**, the desert lizard, was one of the small creatures eaten by the tribes, along with insects and wild plants that entered the diet during periods of famine.

This old proverb becomes understandable if we connect it with the age of the great famine—the famine that the Torah calls the famine of the land of Canaan and that Arabic narrative sources call the famine of Mecca—when tribes were forced to eat insects and wild plants.

This is also clear from early biblical laws that permitted the eating of certain kinds of insects and counted them among acceptable foods.

Brandi expresses surprise at the rock carvings left by the Thamudites:

> “Such an activity—the existence of fishing vessels—cannot be reconciled with the condition of wandering Bedouins.”

He is entirely right.

A life of constant movement in search of food seems impossible to reconcile with fishing boats, which require a measure of settled life. To a reader of conventional history, the combination appears almost incomprehensible.

Seen from another angle, however, it becomes entirely understandable.

The combination of two different food systems gives us a clear picture of the conditions and limits imposed by successive periods of crisis that struck Arabia and forced its tribes to search for new sources of survival.

Within this setting, the fish acquired a ritual role on the table of starving communities.

**Nun** became a deity—a totem—among the earliest Arab tribes.

Later, with the appearance of Christianity, the fish was transformed into a religious symbol and became one of the foundations of the doctrine of salvation.

This, in our view, fully explains the symbolism of the Christian Last Supper and the place of the fish in the sacred meal that fed thousands of hungry people.

In this form, the tribes recovered the memory of a mythical destruction from which humanity had been saved.

From his study of Thamudite personal names such as **Hut**, “fish,” and **Samaka**, also meaning “fish,” Brandi concluded that some relationship must have existed between fishing boats and the appearance of fish in the tribal diet.

But he did not take the next step toward conclusions of greater significance.

Nor did he notice that these names appeared only long after the tribes had survived the great famine, by which time they had become part of an integrated cultural system in which the worship of the god Nun played a central role in the idea of human salvation from suffering and destruction.

The saving god came from the sea carrying a gift.

Moses, Noah, Yunus, and Yunan all follow the same pattern.

Each passes through danger and hardship in order to save a people waiting for the gift to arrive.

To unravel the biblical idea of the saviour, we must therefore reopen the entire complex of myths surrounding the god **Nun**, from which, in our view, that idea developed and within which the figure of Moses himself took shape.

This also requires a careful and systematic return to Arabic historical narratives, which preserve some form of knowledge about the unspoken sources of this ancient Arab belief.

The works of Muslim chroniclers and commentators contain countless references and details bearing, in one way or another, on the interpretation of the Qur’anic verse:

**“Nun, by the pen and what they write.”**

A few examples will be enough: al-Tabari in his *History* (1:259), and al-Thaʿalabi in *Araʾis al-Majalis* (149; 287).

What matters most in reading Surat al-Qalam is the close relationship between its meanings and the way Muslim commentators approached the word **Nun**.

The Qur’anic text says:

> **So be patient with your Lord’s judgement, and do not be like the companion of the fish when he cried out in anguish.**
> —Surat al-Qalam, 47–48

The Qur’an explicitly connects Yunus in the belly of the whale with the theme that appears around the verse **“Nun, by the pen and what they write,”** and with the idea of salvation.

This suggests that **Nun** refers specifically to an older Arab god of writing who came out of the sea after struggling against its dangers and overcoming them in order to bring the gift.

Seen in this way, a close relationship can also be found with the biblical story of the infant Moses being cast into the river, struggling against its danger, emerging from it, and later bringing the Law.

This would represent an elaboration of the same basic idea rather than, as has sometimes been claimed, an imitation of the legend of Sargon I, who likewise says that his priestess mother placed him in a river.

The same line of interpretation can be extended to the symbolic meaning of Christian baptism and the immersion of a child in water.

This ritual belongs to the same underlying structure:

being cast into the water, crossing through it, and later bringing the gift.

It is striking that Coptic, a form of the ancient Egyptian language, records the same word **Nun** as the name of the primordial mass of water.

The Akkadian word **nunu** likewise means both fish and a mass of water.

Ibn Abbas related the myth of Nun in the following form:

> The first thing God created was the Pen. Then He created Nun. Then He spread out the earth.

Muslim jurists debated the meaning of the word for a long time.

Some interpreted **Nun** as an **inkwell**, reasoning from the fact that the Pen came before it in the order of creation.

Ali Fahmi Khashim followed this approach, proposing a relationship between *Nun* and an Egyptian as well as an Arabic word carrying the meaning of inkwell.

In his interpretation of the name **Quraysh**, however, Ibn Abbas gives us an idea consistent with the argument above.

In his view, Quraysh derives from **qarsh**, the shark, rather than from *qurush* in the sense of money and trade, or from the idea of gathering together. Others had claimed that the tribe received its name from *taqarrush*, meaning to gather in one place.

Drawing on Ibn Abbas, however, we believe that the name is closely connected with the existence of a great deity worshipped by starving communities.

Ibn Abbas’s insistence that the tribal name is related to the shark, combined with his insistence that **Nun** means the fish or whale and was the first creature on earth, strongly reinforces this interpretation:

**Nun is the whale; in other words, Thoth, god of knowledge, and the primordial human being, the first fish.**

What, then, is the hidden relationship between the **Pen** and the **fish** in Qur’anic language?

If we go back to the earliest foundational myths, a pattern begins to emerge.

The pairing of the two in the act of creation is difficult to understand without recovering the original idea formed in ancient religion around the god of knowledge.

The first human being was a creature combining man and fish: **N**.

He had to face the terrors of the Flood and the sea and reach dry land in his vessel, just as Noah, Yunus, and Yunan would later do.

He then delivered the message—the **Pen**, meaning religious law and wisdom.

The Qur’anic story in which the whale swallows Yunus, who remains inside it for three days before finally being cast onto dry land, is the same ancient pattern as Noah reaching land and saving humanity from destruction by carrying living creatures with him.

It is also the same pattern as the biblical story of **Yunan**, or Yonah.

Notice the root **nun** in the names Noah, Yunus, and Yonah.

The vessel—the whale—saved humanity and brought it to shore.

This symbolic content finds its fullest expression in the Qur’anic account of the whale saving Yunus.

In the Qur’anic text, the whale does not devour the prophet. It **swallows** him: it hides him within its body and carries him toward dry land.

This symbolic dimension of the struggle also points toward the way of life of the earliest Arab communities in the period after the mythical Flood, when the gods had destroyed everything that existed and human beings were forced into a barren, devastated, inorganic world while waiting for salvation.

For this reason, the Qur’anic name given to Yunus—**Dhu al-Nun**, “the man of the Nun”—is especially suggestive.

God says:

> **And Dhu al-Nun, when he went away in anger...**
> —Surat al-Anbiya, 87

All these close connections—between the deity, the way of life of ancient communities, their violent and tragic struggles, and the linguistic and behavioural similarities among their heroes—become intelligible within the wider mythology of **water, wisdom, and the heavens** in ancient cultures.

Following this parallel line of inquiry, we can read the Qur’anic verse, **“Nun. By the Pen and what they write,”** as referring to one manifestation of the ancient Arab god of knowledge, wisdom, and writing—the Thrice-Great. The close connection between the name **Nun** and the **Pen**, as the symbol of this knowledge, points in that direction.

If we take the argument one step further and examine the later forms in which this figure appears, we find this **N** again in the person of **Noah**, whom all the traditions describe as a prophet. Noah, too, emerged from the heart of the primeval deep and the cosmic flood. He struggled against the sea and overcame it. He carried humanity safely to dry land and brought it a gift: knowledge.

The resemblance between these two ancient heroic or divine figures, Noah and **N**, goes beyond the linguistic resemblance created by their common root. It extends to the nature and purpose of their struggle against the sea—the primeval deep, or flood. If **N** gave humanity knowledge, symbolized by writing, Noah gave humanity practical knowledge: the art of building the **fulk**, the ship.

The idea of humanity’s earliest knowledge was naturally associated with three inseparable things: **wisdom, knowledge, and writing**. This is why ancient Arab mythology, and later Islamic tradition, portrayed **Enoch** as a master of the celestial spheres—that is, of the stars and planets. Biblical, Arab, and Islamic genealogies then made him an ancestor of Noah.

But why did the religious story of Noah become associated with the making of the **fulk**, the ship, while the name of Enoch became associated with the science of the **falak**, the heavens? What connects **fulk**, meaning a ship, with **falak**, meaning the stars, planets, and cosmos?

From the earliest Islamic period, Muslim scholars debated this remarkable similarity in the form and meaning of the two Arabic words **fulk** and **falak**. It attracted so much attention that Ibn Manzur, in *Lisan al-‘Arab* under the entry *falak*, effectively brought the two meanings together, following an old Arab understanding of the word.

The **falak**, he says, can refer to the movement of waves when the sea surges and becomes disturbed. In a similar sense, the **falak** of the heavens refers to their circular movement. The word itself denotes rotation. This is how al-Zajjaj understood the Qur’anic verse:

**“Each is swimming in an orbit.”**
—Surat al-Anbiya’, 33

From the same underlying idea the Arabs formed a description for rounded land: a **falak** was a piece of ground that rose in a circular shape above its surroundings, with the singular form **falaka**. The word survives in everyday Iraqi speech, where it is used for traffic roundabouts.

Once the universe is imagined as an immense sea—and in the Torah the universe becomes a great primeval deep—the stars and planets can be imagined as ships driven by its waves. From the heart of those restless, colliding waters emerged **N** and Noah, each carrying the gift safely to shore.

As happened with so many ancient divine figures, the image of the old god **N** eventually broke into separate forms. At different stages of human development, and in different periods and places, these fragments could reappear in local and tribal traditions under different names while retaining the same underlying meaning.

We will see this more clearly when we reconnect the names and images: **N, Noah, Enoch or Idris, Jonah or Yunus, and Hermes**. All of them, I will argue, developed out of older traditions concerning the Sumerian **Oannes**, the man-fish; the Greek **Oannes**; the Akkadian **Nu-Nu**; and the Egyptian **Thoth**, whose Arabic name itself evokes the *hut*, the great fish.

The *Book of Enoch* does not appear in the Jewish sacred canon. It survives only among the works scholars call the **Apocrypha**, a body of popular or non-canonical biblical literature which, according to widely accepted estimates, reached its written form around the year 100 CE.

It contains remnants of a myth that seems to have come from an ancient Arab source. Versions of the same story repeatedly appear in the books of Arab chroniclers. Yaqut al-Hamawi recorded one version directly from the inhabitants of the island of **Qays** in Bahrain, a thriving port in ancient times.

The story concerns a marriage in the remote past from which a race of giants was born. These giants then carried their civilization across the sea to the peoples of the mainland.

The myth says that:

> **“The beautiful daughters of men married the sons of God.”**

From these unions came a race of giants who migrated through the island of Kish, or Qays, carrying their civilization with them.

The central idea in Yaqut’s account belongs to the same pattern: a mass migration across the sea brings the long-awaited gift of **civilization** to the mainland.

This should make us look more carefully at the extraordinary richness of the myth and at its recurring idea that the earliest civilization on land came from the sea.

From this starting point, which appears again and again in the traditions, we can radically reconsider our understanding of the Flood and of Noah’s emergence from it with the people, animals, and other living beings he carried safely to land, where a new human line could begin.

At the heart of the belief in a universal flood lies the image of the **man-fish emerging from the water**. After the flood, existence itself begins anew. The Qur’an says:

**“And We made from water every living thing.”**
—Surat al-Anbiya’, 30

Every living thing, according to this belief, therefore belongs to water—to the primordial body of water called **Nunu**, which Sumerian mythology portrayed as the first source of creation.

From it emerged the man-fish, Oannes, just as Noah emerged from the Flood.

Noah’s miracle was the making of the **fulk**, the ship that carried him and the other creatures to dry land. His ancestor Enoch, meanwhile, was portrayed in Islamic tradition as the master of the **aflak**, the celestial spheres: the man who understood the stars and planets.

Ibn Hisham writes:

> **“Enoch is Idris the Prophet, as they claim—and God knows best. He was the first of Adam’s descendants to be given prophecy and the first to write with the pen.”**
> —*al-Sira*, 1:5

A much later Muslim scholar, Imam Muhammad ibn Yusuf al-Salihi al-Shami, writes in *The Life of the Best of God’s Servants*, 1:377:

> *Al-Mustadrak* relates through a weak chain of transmission from Wahb ibn Munabbih, may God have mercy on him, that he was asked about Idris. He replied: “He was the ancestor of Noah’s father,” though it is also said that he was Noah’s own ancestor. Al-Hakim says in *al-Mustadrak* that most of the Companions held that Noah came before Idris. He was called Idris because he studied so much of the books of God and the traditions of Islam. He was the first to sew clothes and wear them, whereas before him people wore skins. Idris in Syriac is Enoch, meaning one who worships greatly. He was the first to teach the science of the heavens, arithmetic, and the judgments of the stars.

Al-Salihi’s passage places the identification of Idris with Enoch at the very heart of the main idea running through these myths: the correspondence between the ancient Arab image of the cosmos as a primeval sea in which the stars and planets swim, and their image of the earthly world—what jurists call this present life—as something born from a flood.

Notice also the close resemblance in Arabic between **fulk**, the ship, and **falak**, the celestial sphere.

Seen in this way, Idris—the god of the Pen and of knowledge, and therefore the equivalent of Thoth and Hermes—becomes an ancestor of the mythical Flood hero who saved humanity.

The Greeks, in turn, made Hermes a god of astronomy and the stars. In early Greek belief he rose to the highest rank among the gods, just as Thoth was the Thrice-Great god.

Are all these figures, then, really fragments of a single figure, originally produced by a shared Mediterranean conception of the cosmos, creation, and the world?

Bar Hebraeus, in *A Short History of the Dynasties*, p. 90, suggests that astronomy was created after the Flood by **Cainan son of Arphaxad**. We know almost nothing, however, about this mythical figure.

Ibn al-Athir, in *al-Kamil*, Leiden edition, 1:33, says that the prophet Idris was Enoch son of Jared son of Mahalalel. He was the first to write with a pen and the first to study the stars and arithmetic. The Greek sages, Ibn al-Athir adds, called him **Hermes the Wise**, and held him in great reverence.

Ibn al-Athir’s statement—that Idris was the first person to write with the pen—appears again and again in the writings and discussions of Muslim scholars, both ancient and modern.

It leads us directly back to the Qur’anic phrase:

**“Nun. By the Pen.”**

For among the Arabs, Idris is Enoch; at the same time he is the Greek Hermes; and Hermes, as we have seen, is the Egyptian Thoth. All of these figures ultimately belong to the fragmented image of the Sumerian **Enki**.

Out of this fractured image emerges the story of the prophet **Jonah**—*Yonah* in the Hebrew Bible—which became, in Islamic tradition, the story of the prophet **Yunus**.

Muslim storytellers would relate the story almost as though it were another version of the myth of Noah.

Both figures emerge from the sea or the cosmic deep. Both confront the sea and overcome it. Both are rescued by a fish or whale and carried safely to shore.

The symbolism of the ship and the great fish in these myths, and the fact that their heroes bear names containing the same root, **Nun**, naturally invites comparison with the ancient Sumerian and Akkadian image of **Nun**, the primordial mass of water.

Through the transparent symbolism of the ship and the fish, these mythical heroes travel inside their protective vessels while passing through a violent struggle filled with suffering. Yet they remain tirelessly determined to reach a broken and disappointed humanity, to save it and free it from misery.

This is the inner rhythm that organizes these stories and binds their symbols to one central idea: **the desire to conquer the Flood**, understood as the image of an overwhelming and brutal force.

These myths were undoubtedly told by a maritime people whose collective memory had been deeply shaped by historical experiences of migration across the sea.

The Arabs today scarcely remember that they were, in origin, a maritime people, before the familiar stereotype of the desert Bedouin came to dominate their older image. Yet a careful and systematic return to the Arab traditions that describe the Arabs as a maritime people living along the coasts of the Red Sea and the southern Mediterranean reveals this forgotten side of their past.

The striking recurrence of the same linguistic root in the heroes’ names strongly suggests that these figures—travelling across the sea in ships and inside great fish, struggling on behalf of humanity and seeking its salvation—are all fragments of the same hero.

His task is always to carry the message safely to shore: in other words, to bring **civilization** to a community waiting anxiously for its arrival.

Ultimately this divine hero is the god himself: hidden, all-powerful, mighty, and possessed of eternal wisdom and knowledge, the one:

**“Who taught by the Pen, taught man what he did not know.”**
—Surat al-‘Alaq, 4–5

This is the god **Nun**.

The way in which this ancient, deeply rooted cultural inheritance passed through the transparent framework of genealogical lists is also revealing. The heroes came to belong to a single family tree. They were given blood relationships: one became another’s father, son, or grandson, as we have already seen with Noah and Idris.

Behind these apparently simple genealogies lies the older idea of the **genealogies of the gods**, as the Arabs imagined them and as the Greeks later developed them through the Phoenicians.

Ibn Abbas, may God be pleased with him, interpreted the Qur’anic verse concerning Yunus,

**“And he called out in the darknesses”**
—Surat al-Anbiya’, 87

as referring to:

> **“the darkness of the night, the darkness of the sea, and the darkness of the belly of the whale.”**

Al-Tha‘labi records this in *‘Ara’is al-Majalis*, p. 410.

It is an exceptionally perceptive interpretation. Ibn Abbas brings the different forms of darkness together within the prophet’s struggle against the sea. Yunus emerges victorious from darkness layered upon darkness.

This identification of the darkness of night, the darkness of the sea, and the darkness of the whale’s belly supports a central idea in ancient religious belief: **light is born from the heart of darkness**.

The ancient Egyptians imagined the emergence of Thoth from the head of Seth in much the same way, for Thoth was a lunar god.

Islam itself presented its appearance as an emergence of light from darkness. It addressed the Arabs through this image and gave it lasting form. In doing so, Islam placed itself within a cultural pattern that was already deeply rooted and remained present in the Arab community’s understanding of the world, the cosmos, and its own existence.

Islam became a living embodiment of this symbolic birth of light.

The belief in a struggle against the sea or the Flood can be seen in the oldest surviving myth from Sumer: the struggle of the primordial mass, **Enki**, against **Tiamat**, arranged by the god **Ea**.

This, I believe, provides the real key to a radical and productive understanding of an episode that the Torah presents as both historical and religious: the **parting of the sea and the passage of the Israelites during their departure from Egypt**.

The so-called miracle of the parting of the sea should be seen from this perspective. It repeats the same image of the hero struggling against the sea or the Flood in order to carry a human community safely to shore.

From the perspective of this book, the Exodus of the Israelites was neither a historical event nor, in itself, a religious event. There is no foundation in any period of the history of this community that can confirm the occurrence of such an episode.

A more productive way to understand it is within the broader pattern of great tribal migrations: peoples leaving their homelands.

In the case of the Israelites, we can see the merging of two traditions.

The first concerns the departure of the **Hyksos** from Egypt—that is, the expulsion of the early Arab nomadic groups. This, in my view, was a genuine historical event affecting a number of vanished peoples, including the Amalekites and the Israelites.

The second tradition belongs to the stories of migration, wandering, and the search for a saviour.

The mythical biblical account of Moses’ miracle and the parting of the sea, which forms a central episode in the Book of Exodus (13:17–14:3), is closely connected with this ancient belief in a struggle against the sea.

The earliest Arab communities inherited this belief when they were still maritime peoples and before they gradually moved toward the desert.

It seems clear that a story centred on the sea and the struggle against it could only have arisen among a people who lived near the sea.

The account in Exodus can therefore be understood as a surviving fragment of the buried memory of those vanished tribes: tribes that forgot their maritime past as Bedouin life came to dominate their identity.

The **Book of Jonah**, in its narrative structure, consists of three constructed units or sections. Through them, the storyteller tries to create a unified tale of a bitter struggle against the sea, itself the survival of a buried memory.

From a technical and literary point of view, the division into sections serves mainly to keep the reader focused on the central idea: the prophet’s struggle against the overwhelming force of the sea, his challenge to it, and finally his victory.

The reader moves through the three sections following the development of this struggle, immersed throughout in the same atmosphere of confrontation with roaring waves and dangerous sea creatures.

Several points need to be made here.

The hero of the biblical book is called **Jonah**, while in the Qur’an he is **Yunus**. But the form of his name suggests that he did not originally belong to the Israelites.

Jonah is one of the minor prophets of the Hebrew Bible. He appears late in the prophetic sequence and is treated as one of the later Israelite prophets before the rise of Christianity.

It is therefore possible, I would argue, to show that he had no real historical existence, and that his appearance in biblical tradition instead belongs to the much older tribal stories of the same struggle against the sea.

A reading of the Hebrew text reveals several important details. The hero comes from a maritime setting. Indeed, this is the only biblical book whose atmosphere differs so sharply—and perhaps even contradicts—the familiar desert setting found elsewhere.

Its hero leaves the land and enters the sea in order to deliver a message.

It is difficult to accept the idea of a late prophet emerging from a maritime province that does not appear on the maps of the ancient world and becoming the prophet of a community of migrating tribes at a time when Christianity itself was about to emerge as a major religion from the historical experience of that same community.

This was also an age in which the great Greek and Roman civilizations were flourishing.

Even the name **Jonah** seems to contain an echo that reaches deep into Arabia, particularly the period in which Arab tribes entered the great regional struggle on the side of Persia.

The number **three** recurs throughout the biblical story. In addition to the book’s three main sections, there is the journey of three days connected with the return to land.

The number has a symbolic value that we will later see connected with Christian mysteries, and beyond them with Hermeticism, since Hermes is the **Thrice-Great**, just like Enoch and Thoth.

The number may also be connected in some way with the Egyptian pyramid and its triangular form.

During the Middle Ages, fascination with the Egyptian pyramids and their mysteries spread widely. Europe became obsessed with discovering the meaning of the number three and its possible connection with the pyramid. Even Freemasonry eventually adopted triangular forms and the image of the pyramid in its rituals and symbols.

In its language and composition, the Book of Jonah belongs to what is known as the **Classical period of Hebrew**.

Some biblical scholars believe that it may have been written around the period of the Babylonian exile. Its original material, however, belongs to much older religious beliefs.

Whatever the exact date of composition, from the perspective proposed here the book is a late retelling of an ancient story that has no connection with any actual historical or religious event.

It is a story of the symbolic struggle against the sea and its dangerous creatures, in which the saviour finally triumphs and carries the message safely to dry land.

The first section begins when Jonah hears the word of the Lord:

**“Rise, go to Nineveh, that great city, and cry out against it, for its wickedness has risen up.”**

Jonah tries to escape the task. He flees from the Lord and heads toward a city called **Tarshish**.

According to Yaqut al-Hamawi (2:26), Tarshish was the Greek name for the city of Tunis. The author of *al-Rawd al-Mi‘tar*, p. 144, describes it as the site of an ancient Christian place of worship.

The name of the city toward which Jonah flees therefore appears to have some connection with the Greeks and their ancient settlements along the Mediterranean coast, including the state of Carthage that later challenged Rome.

Logically, of course—and myth by its nature often destroys the logical sequence of events—a journey toward Greek or Roman Tarshish does not lead to Nineveh in Iraq.

During his flight from the mission given to him by the Lord, Jonah finds a ship sailing for Tarshish by way of **Jaffa**.

The presence of the name Jaffa, the Palestinian city, should not mislead us into assuming that Palestine was necessarily the intended point of departure. If it were, the geographical sequence of the story would cease to make sense.

The author of the book, writing around the period of the Babylonian exile, appears to be doing what storytellers commonly do: merging personal or inherited memories with the events he is narrating.

Once Jonah boards the ship, a violent wind rises over the sea and the vessel comes close to sinking. The sailors are terrified and each cries out to his own god.

Jonah, by contrast, is fast asleep deep inside the ship—“in the belly of the ship.”

The sailors therefore begin to suspect that the divine anger directed against them may have been caused by an impure or sinful person among them.

They search and find Jonah sleeping below. The captain asks how he can possibly remain asleep while the ship is being thrown about by the storm.

Jonah appears to have been forced to reveal the secret of his flight.

The sailors then decide to cast lots in order to determine which excess cargo, or which guilty passenger, should be thrown into the sea.

Jonah is ultimately thrown overboard after the casting of lots—but apparently at his own request:

> **“Take me and throw me into the sea, and it will become calm for you, for I know that this great storm has come upon you because of me.”**

For us as readers, the only convincing explanation for Jonah’s strange desire to be thrown into the sea—when he has already fled from the Lord and refused to carry the message—lies in the area that religious texts leave unspoken:

**he must confront the sea itself.**

In the next section of the biblical story, the great fish swallows the man who has come to struggle against the sea:

> **“The Lord appointed a great fish to swallow Jonah, and Jonah was in the belly of the fish for three days and three nights. Jonah prayed to his God from the belly of the fish. Then the Lord commanded the fish, and it cast Jonah onto dry land so that he might travel to Nineveh.”**

The appearance of the name **Nineveh** in this story can readily be connected with the period of the Babylonian captivity, when the memories of peoples defeated or displaced by the Assyrian Empire became gathered around this region, as the biblical texts themselves suggest.

Memories of exile may therefore have been woven into an older narrative about the struggle against the sea.

The important thing, however, is to see the **mythic meaning rather than the geography**.

The struggle against the sea does not involve only fighting its dangerous creatures. It also involves entering or being carried by its gentle creatures in order to save humanity.

In my view, the Book of Jonah revolves around three major themes, all belonging to a single central idea: **crossing the sea, or passing through water, in order to reach dry land**—as in the biblical story of Jacob crossing the ford of Jabbok.

### 1. The struggle between sea and land

This is the original material from which the idea of the Flood develops.

To reach dry land is to reach stability.

That desire lies at the heart of the hopes of migrating tribes, whose constant movement exposes them to the danger of disappearance, fragmentation, and the dispersal of their people and possessions.

To reach land, conquer the sea, and overcome it therefore means **settlement**—and settlement means **civilization**, the result of remaining rooted in one place.

In this sense, the image of the great fish becomes identical in function to the image of the ship: both are means of reaching dry land.

### 2. The struggle between human beings and the monsters of the sea

If we imagine the fish that swallowed the biblical hero—Yonah, or Yunus in Islamic tradition—as a gentle dolphin, a huge but benevolent sea creature, an important symbolic dimension becomes visible.

The dolphin is the innocent opposite of **Leviathan**, the sea monster, as Northrop Frye observes in *Theory of Myths*, pp. 46–49.

In this contrast between the gentle, benevolent dolphin and the savage creatures of the sea, the idea of a human being rescued by an innocent sea creature points toward an ancient pattern in humanity’s earliest struggles.

Frye observes that the dolphin’s gentleness and docility make it a domesticated or friendly creature—in other words, a kind of **totem**.

This totem is organically connected with the underlying struggle between **barrenness and fertility**.

The violent monsters of the sea symbolize barrenness and sterility within the sea itself. Its gentle and benevolent creatures, by contrast, embody fertility—the ancient symbolism of **Nun**, since the *hut* or great fish is large, powerful, and benevolent.

We know from the history of early communities, including Arab tribes such as **Thamud** living along the shores of the Red Sea, that during the repeated famines that struck Arabia they depended heavily on the sea and its fish.

In this sense, **Nun** becomes the innocent opposite of the sea monster in Phoenician belief: **Poseidon**.

Poseidon is the same marine monster who stood at the centre of many Greek myths.

Herodotus doubted that Poseidon was originally a Greek god and believed that the deity had come from outside Greece. Strabo, meanwhile, suggested some connection with the Arabian Gulf.

Once again we therefore find ourselves facing the same idea:

**the struggle of fertility against the barrenness of the sea.**

By preserving Jonah, or Yunus, and protecting him, the dolphin, fish, or **Nun** allows the hero to travel across the sea, confront sterility, overcome it, remove the danger threatening the city, and finally deliver the divine message.

This is how heroes travel in mythical narratives to cities inhabited or threatened by monsters. Their sacred task is to destroy the evil, sterility, and violence threatening those cities.

Early Christian symbolism expresses the same idea with unusual depth.

The miracle of the **two fish** that relieved the hunger of Christ and his followers, when the crowd numbered around five thousand, symbolically embodies the Christian idea of salvation.

It is therefore hardly accidental, in this context, that Christ chose his most devoted followers from among **fishermen**.

Finally, we should note that from the Hyksos period onward the ancient Egyptians worshipped a deity called **Sobek**, whose name, in Egyptian, means “fish.”

### 3. Carrying the message across the sea to dry land

The Book of Jonah shows how this mission unfolds.

It has the force of destiny. Jonah cannot escape it.

He begins as a man fleeing from the enormity of his task: the confrontation with the creatures of the sea. In the Qur’an:

**“And [remember] the Man of the Fish when he went away in anger.”**

Yet the man who is fleeing, or departing in anger, soon chooses a path that leads him directly to the ship—that is, directly to the sea.

This apparently contradictory sense of destiny lies at the heart of the story.

The struggle against sterility is itself the divine mission.

The Qur’anic word **iltaqamahu**, in the verse describing what the fish did to Yunus (Surat al-Saffat, 142), expresses with remarkable precision the mythical identification between the human being and his totem.

The fish, in this reading, does not simply consume him. It holds him within its mouth and later casts him toward the shore.

Here we have, very clearly, the archetypal image of **birth from the mouth**—the birth of the **word**.

In the Qur’an, Christ, the saviour himself, undergoes the same symbolic birth from the word:

**“A Word from Him, whose name is the Messiah.”**
—Surat Al ‘Imran, 45

God begins the act with a word.

Elsewhere in the Qur’an, God sends a word to Mary and then gives that word human form. Muslim scholars generally agreed that the “word” in this passage refers to the child who is born.

Humanity, then, is born out of the primeval deep, the sea, or the primordial mass of water so that the message may be carried safely to dry land.

The symbolic image of the fish casting Yunus or Jonah from its mouth onto the shore is the same image of symbolic birth from the mouth.

He, too, is a **word**.

Within this wide field of meaning, the significance becomes clearer when we remember that **Thoth was the god of wisdom**.

The way this ancient cultural inheritance penetrated the seemingly transparent structure of genealogical lists—turning the heroes into members of a single family tree, binding them through blood, and making one the father, son, or grandson of another, as with Noah and Idris—points, in its simplest form, to the old idea of **divine genealogies**, as the Arabs imagined them and as the Greeks later developed them through the Phoenicians.

Ibn Abbas, may God be pleased with him, interpreted the Qur’anic description of Yunus,

**“Then he called out in the darknesses”**
—Surat al-Anbiya’, 87

as meaning:

> **“the darkness of night, the darkness of the sea, and the darkness of the belly of the whale.”**

Al-Tha‘labi records this in *‘Ara’is al-Majalis*, p. 410.

It is an exceptionally intelligent and precise interpretation.

Ibn Abbas identifies several forms of darkness within the struggle against the sea. Yunus emerges victorious from darkness layered upon darkness.

This interpretation, in which the darkness of night, the darkness of the sea, and the darkness of the whale’s belly become equivalent images, supports a central idea in ancient religion: **light emerging from the very heart of darkness**.

The ancient Egyptians imagined Thoth emerging from the head of Seth in much the same way, as a lunar deity.

Islam likewise understood itself as the emergence of light from darkness. It addressed the Arabs through this idea and firmly established it, placing itself within a cultural current that remained deeply embedded in the Arab community’s understanding of the world, the cosmos, and its own existence.

Islam became a living embodiment of this symbolic birth of light.

The belief in a struggle against the sea or the Flood, expressed in the earliest surviving Sumerian myth—the struggle of the primordial mass **Enki** against **Tiamat**, under the direction of the god **Ea**—provides the real key, I believe, to a radical and productive understanding of something the Torah presents as a historical and religious event: the parting of the sea and the crossing of the Israelites during their departure from Egypt.

The so-called miracle of the divided sea and the Israelite crossing should be understood from this perspective.

It repeats the same image of the hero confronting the sea or the Flood so that a human community can reach the shore.

From the perspective developed in this book, the Israelite Exodus was neither a historical event nor a religious event in the form in which it is presented. There is no basis in any known period of this community’s history that can establish the occurrence of such an event.

It makes more sense to place the story within the wider context of great tribal migrations: peoples leaving their homelands.

In the case of the Israelites, two different traditions seem to have been combined.

One concerns the departure of the **Hyksos from Egypt**—the expulsion of the earliest Arab nomadic groups. This was, in my view, a genuine historical event involving a number of peoples who later disappeared, including the Amalekites and the Israelites.

The other belongs to the traditions of migration, wandering, and the search for a saviour.

The biblical myth of Moses parting the sea, which occupies a central place in Exodus 13:17–14:3, is closely connected with the belief in the struggle against the sea inherited by early Arab communities when they were maritime peoples and before they gradually moved toward the desert.

A story about the sea and the struggle against it could only have been told by a community that lived beside it.

The Exodus narrative is therefore, in this reading, a surviving fragment of the buried memory of vanished tribes that forgot their maritime past and came to live deeply within a Bedouin way of life.

The Book of Jonah is constructed from three narrative units or sections. The storyteller uses them to create a single story about a bitter struggle against the sea, itself a buried memory.

From a technical literary standpoint, this division serves mainly to keep the reader fixed upon the central idea: the prophet’s struggle against the overwhelming power of the sea, his challenge to it, and his eventual victory.

The reader passes from one unit to another intent on following each new stage of the confrontation, remaining immersed in the same atmosphere of struggle with roaring waves and dangerous sea creatures.

A few observations are necessary here.

The hero of the biblical book is called **Jonah**, while the Qur’an calls him **Yunus**. Yet the form of the name suggests that he did not originally belong to the Israelites.

Because Jonah is classed among the minor biblical prophets and appears near the bottom of the prophetic sequence—one of the later Israelite prophets before the emergence of Christianity—it is possible, I would argue, to show that no historical person lies behind him.

His appearance in the biblical traditions instead belongs to the stories told by ancient tribes and communities about this same struggle against the sea.

A reading of the Hebrew text reveals several things. Among them is the fact that its hero comes from a maritime setting.

Indeed, this is the only biblical book whose atmosphere is substantially different from, and perhaps even opposed to, the familiar atmosphere of nomadic life found elsewhere in the text.

Its hero travels from land to sea in order to carry a message.

It is difficult to accept the idea of a late prophet from a maritime province that appears nowhere on the maps of the ancient world becoming the prophet of a community of migrating tribes at a time when Christianity was already close to emerging as a major religion from the historical experience of that same community.

This was, moreover, an age of flourishing Greek and Roman civilizations and of immense change across the ancient world.

The name **Jonah** itself seems to contain an echo reaching far into Arabia, at the moment when Arab tribes entered this wider conflict on Persia’s side.

The number **three** repeatedly appears in the biblical story.

The book itself has three sections. There is also the journey of three days associated with reaching land.

This number possesses a symbolic importance that we will see connected with Christian mysteries and, beyond them, with Hermeticism, since Hermes is the **Thrice-Great**, just as Enoch and Thoth are.

The number three may also have some relationship to the Egyptian pyramid and its triangular form.

During the Middle Ages, fascination with the Egyptian pyramids and their mysteries spread widely. Europe became obsessed with discovering the meaning of the number three and its supposed connection with the Egyptian pyramid.

Eventually Freemasonry itself adopted the triangle and the image of the pyramid in its rituals and symbols.

In its language and composition, the Book of Jonah belongs to what is known as the **Classical period of Hebrew**.

Some biblical scholars believe that the book may have been written during the period associated with the Babylonian exile.

Its underlying material, however, reaches back to much older religious beliefs.

Whatever its precise date, from the perspective proposed here it is a late version of an ancient story with no connection to a particular historical or religious event.

It is a story about the symbolic struggle against the sea and its dangerous creatures, in which the saviour finally triumphs and brings the message safely to shore.

The first section opens when Jonah hears the word of the Lord:

**“Rise, go to Nineveh, that great city, and proclaim against it, for its wickedness has risen up.”**

Jonah tries to escape the task and flees from the Lord toward a city called **Tarshish**.

According to Yaqut al-Hamawi, 2:26, Tarshish was the Greek name for the city of Tunis. According to the author of *al-Rawd al-Mi‘tar*, p. 144, it was the site of an ancient Christian place of worship.

The name of the city toward which Jonah fled therefore plainly has some connection with the Greeks and their ancient settlements along the Mediterranean coast, including the Carthaginian state that later challenged Rome.

From a logical standpoint—and myths, by the very nature of their narrative machinery, often break apart the logical sequence of events—flight toward Greek or Roman Tarshish does not lead to Nineveh in Iraq.

As Jonah flees from the mission to which the Lord has called him, he finds a ship bound for Tarshish by way of Jaffa.

The name **Jaffa**, the Palestinian city, should not deceive us into assuming that Palestine is necessarily the point of departure intended by the story, since the geographical sequence would then make little sense.

The author of the book, writing during the period of the Babylonian exile, is doing something traditional and familiar: merging personal and inherited memories with the events he is narrating.

Once Jonah boards the ship, a violent wind rises over the sea and the vessel is close to breaking apart.

The sailors become terrified and each cries out to his own god.

Jonah, however, does the opposite. He is sunk in sleep inside the ship, **“in the belly of the ship.”**

The sailors therefore come to believe that the divine anger threatening them may have been caused by the presence of some impure or guilty person.

They search until they find Jonah sleeping below.

The captain asks him how he can possibly remain asleep while the winds are tossing the ship about.

Jonah apparently has no choice but to reveal the secret of his flight.

The sailors then decide to cast lots to determine which excess cargo, or which sinful passenger, should be thrown into the sea.

Jonah is eventually thrown overboard after the casting of lots—but at his own request:

> **“Take me and throw me into the sea, and it will become calm for you, for I know that this great storm has come upon you because of me.”**

For us as readers, the only convincing explanation for this strange wish on the part of Jonah—who has fled from the Lord and refused to carry the message, yet now asks to be cast into the sea in order to quiet its violence—lies in the unspoken territory of religious texts:

**the confrontation with the sea itself.**

In the next section of the biblical story, the great fish swallows the man who has come to struggle against the sea:

> **“The Lord appointed a great fish to swallow Jonah. Jonah was in the belly of the fish for three days and three nights. Jonah prayed to his God from the belly of the fish. The Lord then commanded the fish to cast Jonah onto dry land so that he might travel to Nineveh.”**

The appearance of the name Nineveh in the story can easily be attributed to memories belonging to the period of the Babylonian captivity, when prisoners from tribes that had rebelled against the Assyrian Empire were gathered in this region, as the biblical texts themselves indicate.

Memories of captivity were therefore woven into the narrative fabric of the older struggle against the sea.

But what matters is the **mythic meaning rather than the geographical one**.

To struggle against the sea does not mean only fighting its dangerous monsters. It also means entering the bodies of its gentle creatures in order to be saved.

In my view, this book revolves around three major themes, all organized around one central idea: the belief in crossing the sea, or passing through water, to reach dry land, as in the biblical account of Jacob crossing the ford of Jabbok.

### 1. The struggle between the sea and dry land

This is the original material behind the idea of the Flood.

Reaching dry land means reaching stability.

That aspiration lies at the heart of the experience of migrating tribes, which live under the constant threat of extinction through wandering, fragmentation, and the dispersal of their people and possessions.

To reach dry land, defeat the sea, and overcome it is therefore to achieve settlement.

Settlement, in turn, means **civilization**, since civilization is ultimately the result of remaining on the land.

In this sense, the great fish and the ship become equivalent images: both are vehicles for reaching shore.

### 2. The struggle between human beings and the monsters of the sea

If we imagine the great fish that swallowed the biblical hero—**Yonah**, Yunus in Islamic tradition, with the final *s* in *Yunus* being a Greek suffix and the original name being *Yun*—as a gentle dolphin, a large but benevolent fish, an important symbolic dimension emerges.

The dolphin is the innocent counterpart of **Leviathan**, the sea monster, as Northrop Frye observes in *Theory of Myths*, pp. 46–49.

Within this contrast between the gentle, benevolent dolphin and the violent, savage creatures of the sea, the image of an innocent fish rescuing a human being points toward an ancient pattern of humanity’s earliest struggles.

Frye notes that the dolphin’s gentleness and submissiveness make it a friendly creature—that is, a **totem**.

This totem is organically connected to the deeper struggle between **barrenness and fertility**.

The violent creatures of the sea are symbolic expressions of barrenness and sterility within the sea, while gentle and benevolent animals embody fertility.

They therefore carry the ancient symbolism of **Nun**, because the *hut* is a great fish: large, powerful, and benevolent.

We know from the history of early communities, including Arab tribes such as Thamud living along the Red Sea coast, that during the successive famines that struck Arabia they survived on the wealth of fish provided by the sea.

In this sense, **Nun** becomes the innocent opposite of the sea monster of Phoenician belief: **Poseidon**, the same marine monster who became central to Greek mythology.

Herodotus doubted that the sea god Poseidon was originally Greek, believing that he had come from somewhere outside Greece. Strabo suggested that he might have some connection with the Arabian Gulf.

Once again, therefore, we find ourselves facing the same idea:

**fertility struggling against the barrenness of the sea.**

By preserving Jonah, or Yunus, and protecting him, the dolphin, whale, or **Nun** allows the hero to travel across the sea, confront sterility, overcome it, remove the danger threatening the city, and deliver the divine message.

This is how heroes travel in mythical narratives to the cities of monsters and kill them.

Their sacred mission is to defeat the evil, sterility, and violence threatening human settlements.

Early Christian symbolism expresses the same idea with unusual depth.

The miracle of the **two fish** that fed Christ and his followers when the crowd numbered around five thousand is a symbolic embodiment of Christian salvation.

It is therefore meaningful, in this context, that Christ chose his devoted followers from among fishermen.

One final point should be noted: from the Hyksos period onward, the ancient Egyptians worshipped a deity called **Sobek**, whose name in Egyptian means “fish.”

### 3. Carrying the message across the sea to dry land

The Book of Jonah reveals how this mission takes place.

It has the force of fate and cannot be escaped.

Jonah begins as a fugitive from the enormity of his task—the struggle against the creatures of the sea.

The Qur’an says:

**“And the Man of the Fish, when he went away in anger.”**

Yet this man, although fleeing or angry, quickly makes his own way toward a ship, and therefore toward the sea itself.

This apparently contradictory sense of destiny lies at the heart of the story.

The struggle against sterility is the divine message.

The Qur’anic word **iltaqamahu**, used to describe what the fish did to Yunus in Surat al-Saffat, 142, expresses with remarkable precision the mythical identification between the human being and his totem.

The fish did not, in this reading, consume him. It held him in its mouth and then cast him onto the shore.

This is very clearly the archetypal image of **birth from the mouth**—the birth of the **word**.

In the Qur’an, Christ, the saviour himself, is born through the same image, because he is a word:

**“A Word from Him, whose name is the Messiah.”**
—Surat Al ‘Imran, 45

God begins His act with a word.

The Qur’an likewise says that God cast a word to Mary and then made the word into a human being. Muslim scholars agreed that the “word” in this passage refers to the child born in this way.

Humanity therefore emerges from the primeval deep, the sea, or the primordial mass of water so that the message can be carried to dry land.

The image of the fish casting Yunus or Jonah from its mouth onto the shore is the same symbolic image of birth from the mouth.

He, too, is a **word**.

Within this broad field of meanings, the significance becomes easier to see when we remember that **Thoth was the god of wisdom**.

# Notes

**1)** In ancient Arab traditions and in the Torah, **Qidma** is a son of Ishmael, who is regarded as the father of all the Arabs. The original form of the name **Quds**, or **Cadmus**, is therefore Qidma, while the final *s* is a Greek suffix. See the Arab genealogical lists in al-Fakihi and al-Azraqi, and Genesis in the Torah.

**2)** This leads us back to the Qur’anic verse describing the cow that the Israelites were instructed to offer as a sacrifice.

**3)** Surat al-Nahr (24).

**4)** According to al-Amidi in *al-Mu’talif wa-l-Mukhtalif* and al-Baghdadi in *Khizanat al-Adab*, there were seventeen ancient poets bearing the name **al-A‘sha**. Among them was the last al-A‘sha, the pre-Islamic poet, whose historical existence is firmly established.

**5)** In my view, the name **Nisus** conceals a derivation of the name **al-Nadira** introduced by the Arab narrator.

**6)** Even today, the Arabs describe every treacherous man as an **Abu Righal**.

**7)** See our treatment of this ancient Arab tradition in *Fire and the Sceptre*, Riad El-Rayyes Publishing.

**8)** This famous Greek myth revolves around the idea of killing the father, or a symbolic substitute for him. In the case of King Minos, his daughter’s lover does not kill the king himself but kills the monster, the symbolic substitute for the father and king. At another level, the myth focuses on the daughter’s betrayal of her father and therefore her violation of the bonds of kinship.

**9)** Present-day Kufa, in Iraq.

**10)** Surat al-Tahrim (5).

**11)** According to *Lisan al-‘Arab*, al-Zajjaj said: “All scholars of interpretation and language understand *al-sa’ihun* to mean those who fast.” He adds that al-Hasan understood it to mean those who perform the obligatory fast, while another view held that it referred to those who fast continually, as described in the earlier scriptures.

**12)** Surat al-Tawbah (2).

**13)** It was also said that a fasting person was called a **sa’ih**, or wanderer, because someone who travels in worship without provisions eats only when food becomes available. Ibn Abbas, may God be pleased with him, was asked about the *sa’ihun* and replied: “Those who fast.” See *Lisan al-‘Arab*.

**14)** According to al-Tabari’s account, Ibn al-Kalbi said: “Qasir was the first person to make the *ghara’ir* [sacks].” We will see later how Ibn al-Kalbi constructed some of his reports from mythology.

**15)** Even today, in everyday Arabic, we still use the originally Akkadian and later Aramaic word **bisht** to mean bad, corrupt, or evil. One might say: “So-and-so is *bisht*.”

**16)** Al-Hira was founded in ancient times and was already well known during the reign of the Akkadian ruler Naram-Sin (2223–2270 BCE).

**17)** Surat Ta Ha (20).

**18)** “Yamanat, its Arabs, Tawdam and Tihamat,” according to the inscription known as **Philby 227**, discovered by Philby in the region of Ma’sal al-Jumh.

**19)** To this day, a place in Yemen preserves a strong trace of the name: **Darb As‘ad al-Kamil**.

**20)** The sequence of al-Tabari traditions that we have examined and continue to examine includes the legend of al-Hadr, the Reward of Sinimmar, and Jadhima.

**21)** In Greek sources the name appears as **Dioscurides**.

**22)** Taha Baqir, *The Epic of Gilgamesh*, Baghdad, p. 48.

**23)** The editor’s note says: “What he means, and God knows best, is the violation by Quraysh of the prohibition against fighting there.” In my view, the editor’s interpretation only made an already difficult passage more obscure.

**24)** Al-Ya‘qubi, *History*; Ibn Qutayba, *Poetry and the Poets*; al-Isfahani, *al-Aghani*; Ibn al-Athir, *al-Kamil*; and Yaqut al-Hamawi, *Mu‘jam al-Buldan*.

**25)** That is, confined in prison.

**26)** Surat al-Fatiha (2).

**27)** The name appears in this form in Sumerian epics and myths as a god of grain (**Apachnan**). There may therefore be some connection between this Sumerian deity and the title of the Hyksos king.

**28)** We should remember that Imru’ al-Qays travelled to Byzantium and fell in love with the emperor’s daughter.

**29)** In the legend of Imru’ al-Qays, we likewise find him fighting to rescue his wife Hind from her abductors.

**30)** *Diwan*, p. 118.
